[Exams] AWS Certified Machine Learning Engineer MLA-C01
Practice Exam 2 - AWS Certified Machine Learning Engineer (MLA-C01) - Results
Attempt 1

Question 1Skipped

A data science team at NovaSoft Systems, a mid-size enterprise software vendor, is evaluating model design choices for a new predictive analytics module embedded in their customer-facing business intelligence platform. 


The team is considering the use of transparent and explainable machine learning models.


Which benefits might persuade a developer to choose this approach? (Select TWO.)
They enhance security by concealing model logic
Explanation
Incorrect. Concealing model logic is a characteristic of opaque or black-box models, not transparent ones. Transparent and explainable models reveal their internal decision-making process by design, which is fundamentally at odds with the concept of security through obscurity. In fact, exposing model logic can introduce intellectual property concerns rather than mitigate them, making this claim technically inaccurate in both directions. Keyword tip: When a distractor frames transparency as a security mechanism, recognize that opacity — associated with ensemble methods or deep neural networks — is the characteristic being conflated, not interpretability.
They simplify the integration process with other systems
Explanation
Incorrect. The ease of integrating a model with other systems depends on architectural choices, API compatibility, and infrastructure design — not on whether the model is transparent or opaque. Transparency is a property of the model's internal logic and decision-making process, entirely independent of how the model is exposed as a service or connected to downstream systems. Keyword tip: When a distractor references 'integration,' 'API compatibility,' or 'interoperability,' recognize that these concerns belong to deployment architecture and are orthogonal to model interpretability.
Correct selection
They foster trust and confidence in model predictions
Explanation
Correct. When stakeholders, auditors, product managers, and end users can understand the rationale behind a model's predictions, it increases confidence in and acceptance of those outputs. For an enterprise software vendor embedding ML into a customer-facing platform, explainability is critical to gaining user adoption and satisfying enterprise procurement requirements that increasingly mandate model auditability. Tools such as Amazon SageMaker Clarify support this need by generating feature attribution reports using SHAP values. Keyword tip: Keywords such as 'trust,' 'stakeholder acceptance,' 'auditability,' and 'high-stakes decisions' are reliable signals that explainability is the core benefit being tested, particularly when the question involves customer-facing or regulated contexts.
They require less computational power and storage
Explanation
Incorrect. Computational and storage requirements are determined by model architecture, dataset size, and algorithmic complexity — not by the model's level of transparency. Both interpretable models such as linear regression and opaque models such as deep neural networks can vary significantly in resource consumption, and many interpretable models are in fact computationally inexpensive, but this is a coincidental property, not a guaranteed benefit of transparency. Keyword tip: When a distractor links model explainability to resource efficiency, treat these as orthogonal properties; MLA-C01 tests awareness that transparency is about decision visibility, not computational cost.
Correct selection
They facilitate easier debugging and optimization
Explanation
Correct. Transparent models expose how inputs are mapped to outputs, enabling developers to trace predictions back to specific features or logic paths. This visibility makes it significantly easier to identify errors, detect biases, and optimize model behavior without relying on indirect probing techniques. In enterprise software contexts, this capability shortens iteration cycles during model development and maintenance. Keyword tip: When a question references 'debugging,' 'optimization,' or 'interpretability' in the context of model design, these signals point to transparent and explainable models as the appropriate choice.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests understanding of model interpretability and explainability in machine learning engineering.

It focuses on recognizing the practical benefits of transparent and explainable models, especially for debugging, optimization, stakeholder trust, auditability, and enterprise adoption.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    They facilitate easier debugging and optimization: This is a correct option. Transparent models make it easier to understand how inputs influence outputs. This helps developers trace prediction behavior, identify errors, detect bias, and improve model logic.

    They enhance security by concealing model logic: This option is incorrect. Transparent models reveal decision logic rather than conceal it. Security through obscurity is not the purpose of interpretability, and explainability can even introduce intellectual property considerations if model logic is exposed too broadly.

    They foster trust and confidence in model predictions: This is a correct option. When stakeholders can understand why a model made a prediction, they are more likely to trust and accept the result. This is especially important in customer-facing, regulated, or high-stakes ML systems.

    They simplify the integration process with other systems: This option is incorrect. Integration depends on APIs, infrastructure, data contracts, deployment patterns, and system architecture. It is not primarily determined by whether the model is transparent or opaque.

    They require less computational power and storage: This option is incorrect. Compute and storage requirements depend on model architecture, data size, and algorithmic complexity. Transparency does not automatically guarantee lower infrastructure requirements.

Why easier debugging and stakeholder trust fit this scenario.

Transparent and explainable models help teams understand how predictions are produced.

This is valuable during model development because engineers can inspect which features are driving predictions.

That visibility makes debugging easier.

For example, if a model makes unexpected predictions, the team can investigate whether the issue is caused by incorrect features, biased training data, leakage, poor preprocessing, or flawed model assumptions.

Explainability also helps with optimization.

When teams understand which inputs influence the model most, they can improve feature engineering, remove noisy features, tune model behavior, and validate whether the model is learning meaningful patterns.

The second major benefit is trust.

Stakeholders are more likely to accept ML outputs when they can understand the reasoning behind predictions.

This matters for product managers, auditors, customers, compliance teams, and business leaders.

In enterprise environments, explainability often supports model governance, procurement reviews, auditability, and responsible AI requirements.

Amazon SageMaker Clarify supports this type of explainability by helping generate feature attribution insights, including SHAP-based explanations.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Debugging: Trace predictions back to influential features.

    Optimization: Improve features, assumptions, and model behavior.

    Trust: Make model decisions easier for stakeholders to understand.

    Auditability: Support review of model behavior in enterprise or regulated environments.

    Bias detection: Help identify problematic patterns in model decisions.

    Responsible AI: Provide visibility into how the model reaches predictions.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Interpretability + debugging + stakeholder trust + auditability = transparent or explainable models.

Remember:

    Interpretability: The model behavior is understandable by design or inspection.

    Explainability: Techniques explain how a model arrived at predictions.

    SageMaker Clarify: AWS service associated with bias detection and model explainability.

    SHAP values: Feature attribution method used to explain predictions.

    Security by concealment: Not a benefit of transparent models.

    System integration: Depends on architecture, not model transparency.

Official AWS references.

    Interpretability versus explainability.

    Amazon SageMaker Clarify model explainability.

Key exam terms: model interpretability, explainability, Amazon SageMaker Clarify, SHAP values, transparent models, stakeholder trust, bias detection, model auditability.

ML Model Development
Question 2Skipped

You are an ML Engineer working for AeroShield Defense Systems, an aerospace and defense contractor that uses a machine learning model to recommend personalized maintenance schedules for mission-critical avionics and propulsion assets.

The model is deployed on Amazon SageMaker and is essential to the company's operations, as any incorrect predictions could have significant operational and safety consequences.


A new version of the model has been developed, and you need to deploy it in production. However, you want to ensure that the deployment process is robust, allowing you to quickly roll back to the previous version if any issues arise.


Additionally, you need to maintain version control for future updates and manage traffic between different model versions. 


Which of the following strategies should you implement to ensure a smooth and reliable deployment of the new model version using Amazon SageMaker, considering best practices for versioning and rollback strategies? (Select TWO.) 
Deploy the new model version immediately and redirect 100% of traffic to it, assuming it has been thoroughly tested and will not require a rollback.
Explanation
Incorrect. Redirecting 100% of traffic immediately to a new model version — even after thorough testing — eliminates any safety net for production issues that were not surfaced during evaluation. In high-stakes environments such as aerospace and defense, where model errors can affect mission readiness and operational safety, this approach is unacceptable because any unforeseen failure would immediately affect all inference traffic with no automated mitigation path. Best practices mandate gradual traffic shifting with continuous monitoring and automated rollback capability. Keyword tip: any option suggesting 'immediate 100% traffic cutover' without a rollback mechanism is always incorrect in MLA-C01 deployment safety scenarios.
Correct selection
Use Amazon SageMaker's built-in versioning to manage different versions of the model, and deploy the new version in a canary release by redirecting a small percentage of traffic to it initially.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker's model registry supports built-in versioning, enabling you to track, approve, and deploy different model iterations with full auditability. Combining this with a canary release deployment — where only a small percentage of production traffic is initially routed to the new model version — allows you to validate real-world performance with minimal blast radius. If issues are detected, traffic can be redirected back to the stable version without impacting the majority of mission-critical inference requests. Keyword tip: when the scenario emphasizes 'version control' combined with 'limited initial traffic exposure' in a high-stakes workload, canary release plus SageMaker model registry is the correct pattern.
Correct selection
Utilize Amazon SageMaker's blue/green deployment strategy to shift traffic gradually from the old model to the new one, ensuring that you can monitor performance and quickly revert if needed.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker's blue/green deployment strategy is a best practice for production model updates. It provisions a new fleet (green) with the updated model while the original fleet (blue) continues serving traffic. SageMaker then gradually shifts traffic to the green fleet during a configurable baking period, during which Amazon CloudWatch alarms can be set to detect anomalies. If any alarm is triggered, SageMaker automatically initiates a rollback to the blue fleet, preserving service availability and minimizing risk — a critical requirement in aerospace and defense environments where model failures can have serious safety consequences. Keyword tip: when you see 'gradual traffic shift,' 'auto-rollback,' and 'baking period' in an MLA-C01 scenario, blue/green deployment on Amazon SageMaker is the target answer.
Deploy the new model version alongside the current one, and use Amazon SageMaker's multi-model endpoint to serve both models simultaneously, splitting traffic between them.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker multi-model endpoints (MMEs) are designed to host multiple independent, unrelated models behind a single endpoint in order to optimize resource utilization and reduce infrastructure cost — typically applied when models share the same framework and container image. MMEs are not intended as a traffic management or controlled rollout mechanism between versions of the same model, and they do not provide the automated rollback or traffic-shifting guardrails required in this scenario. Using an MME for deployment risk management conflates two architecturally distinct use cases. Keyword tip: associate 'multi-model endpoint' with cost optimization and multi-tenant model serving, not with blue/green or canary deployment patterns for version management.
Create a backup of the current model, deploy the new version, and if any issues arise, manually roll back by redeploying the previous model version.
Explanation
Incorrect. While creating a manual backup and redeploying a previous version is technically feasible, it is not a reliable or efficient rollback strategy in a production ML environment. Manual processes introduce human error, increase recovery time, and can lead to extended downtime — particularly unacceptable in safety-critical aerospace and defense contexts. SageMaker's built-in deployment guardrails (blue/green, canary) provide automated rollback mechanisms with CloudWatch alarm integration that are far superior to any manual redeployment workflow. Keyword tip: in MLA-C01 scenarios, options describing 'manual rollback by redeploying' are distractors whenever automated SageMaker deployment guardrails are available.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon SageMaker deployment guardrails and safe model update strategies for production ML systems.

It focuses on using controlled rollout patterns such as blue/green deployment and canary releases when model updates require monitoring, traffic shifting, version control, and rollback capability.

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Utilize Amazon SageMaker's blue/green deployment strategy to shift traffic gradually from the old model to the new one, ensuring that you can monitor performance and quickly revert if needed: This is a correct option. Blue/green deployment creates a new fleet for the updated model while the existing fleet continues serving traffic. SageMaker can shift traffic gradually and use Amazon CloudWatch alarms during the baking period to trigger rollback if issues occur.

    Use Amazon SageMaker's built-in versioning to manage different versions of the model, and deploy the new version in a canary release by redirecting a small percentage of traffic to it initially: This is a correct option. Canary releases expose only a small percentage of production traffic to the new model version first. This reduces blast radius and allows the team to validate real-world behavior before a full rollout.

    Create a backup of the current model, deploy the new version, and if any issues arise, manually roll back by redeploying the previous model version: This option is not ideal for production ML systems. Manual rollback increases recovery time, introduces human error, and does not provide the automated safeguards expected in safety-critical environments.

    Deploy the new model version alongside the current one, and use Amazon SageMaker's multi-model endpoint to serve both models simultaneously, splitting traffic between them: Multi-model endpoints are designed to host multiple models on shared infrastructure for cost efficiency. They are not the correct traffic-shifting or rollback mechanism for controlled deployment of a new version of the same model.

    Deploy the new model version immediately and redirect 100% of traffic to it, assuming it has been thoroughly tested and will not require a rollback: This option is too risky. Even well-tested models can fail under production traffic. In high-stakes environments, traffic should be shifted gradually with monitoring and rollback capability.

Why blue/green deployment and canary release fit this scenario.

The scenario involves a production model update in a high-stakes environment.

That means the deployment strategy must minimize risk.

A new model version should not receive all production traffic immediately.

Instead, the team should use a controlled rollout strategy.

SageMaker blue/green deployment is designed for this.

It keeps the old model fleet available while a new fleet is created for the updated model.

Traffic can then be shifted gradually from the blue fleet to the green fleet.

During the baking period, CloudWatch alarms can monitor operational or model-related metrics.

If an alarm is triggered, SageMaker can roll traffic back to the previous fleet.

A canary release is also appropriate.

In a canary release, only a small percentage of traffic is initially routed to the new model version.

This allows the team to validate behavior under real production conditions while limiting the impact of any failure.

SageMaker model versioning and registry capabilities also support governance, approval, traceability, and rollback planning.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Safe production deployment: Use controlled traffic shifting.

    Rollback readiness: Keep the previous version available.

    Automated protection: Use CloudWatch alarms and SageMaker deployment guardrails.

    Limited blast radius: Use canary traffic exposure.

    Auditability: Track model versions and approval state.

    High-stakes workload protection: Avoid immediate 100% traffic cutover.

Exam tip.

The key exam signals are:

Gradual traffic shift + baking period + CloudWatch alarms + automated rollback = SageMaker blue/green deployment.

Small initial traffic percentage + version control + production validation = canary release.

Remember:

    Blue/green deployment: Runs old and new fleets and shifts traffic safely.

    Canary release: Sends a small percentage of traffic to the new version first.

    CloudWatch alarms: Used to detect issues during deployment.

    Automated rollback: Reduces recovery time and operational risk.

    Model Registry: Supports versioning, approval, and governance.

    Multi-model endpoint: Cost-efficient hosting for multiple models, not a deployment rollback strategy.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon SageMaker blue/green deployment guardrails.

    Amazon SageMaker rolling deployment guardrails.

    Amazon SageMaker multi-model endpoints.

Key exam terms: SageMaker deployment guardrails, blue/green deployment, canary release, model registry, automated rollback, CloudWatch alarms, baking period, traffic shifting.

Question 3Skipped

A telecommunications provider, TelcoNexus, manages a large dataset containing customer and vendor records stored in Amazon S3. The dataset includes information such as contact names, addresses, and phone numbers, but it contains duplicate records due to inconsistencies in data entry.

Some duplicates are exact matches, while others have slight variations (e.g., spelling errors in names or incomplete addresses).

The organization needs to identify and group duplicate records efficiently while ensuring minimal code development to streamline the deduplication process. 

Which solution on AWS will detect duplicates in the dataset with the LEAST operational overhead?
Use SageMaker Data Wrangler to detect and group duplicate records in the dataset by leveraging its data preparation and transformation features.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler is a powerful tool for interactive data preparation, cleaning, visualization, and feature transformation prior to model training. However, it does not provide built-in ML-powered capabilities for detecting or grouping duplicate records, particularly those involving fuzzy matching on text fields such as names and addresses. It is better suited for feature engineering workflows rather than entity resolution or record deduplication tasks. Keyword tip: SageMaker Data Wrangler targets feature transformation and exploratory data analysis (EDA) — do not confuse its general data preparation scope with the record linkage and fuzzy deduplication capability of AWS Glue FindMatches.
Use Amazon EMR with Apache Spark to write a custom deduplication script using SparkSQL.
Explanation
Incorrect. While Amazon EMR with Apache Spark and SparkSQL is a highly capable distributed processing framework suitable for large-scale data transformations, writing a custom deduplication script introduces significant development effort and operational overhead, including cluster provisioning, script maintenance, and performance tuning. This approach does not align with the requirement for minimal code development and least operational overhead. Keyword tip: Amazon EMR with custom scripts implies high operational overhead — use it when fine-grained control is required or when no managed, ML-based alternative exists for the task.
Correct answer
Use AWS Glue FindMatches to automatically detect and group duplicate records in the dataset.
Explanation
Correct. AWS Glue FindMatches is a built-in machine learning transform specifically designed to detect and group duplicate or matching records in datasets, even when the records are not exact matches. It handles fuzzy matching across key attributes such as names, addresses, and phone numbers without requiring custom code. It operates in a serverless fashion and integrates natively with Amazon S3 and the AWS Glue Data Catalog, making it the lowest-overhead solution for ML-powered deduplication at scale. Keyword tip: when the scenario mentions near-duplicate detection, fuzzy matching on contact fields, and minimal coding, AWS Glue FindMatches is the canonical MLA-C01 answer for record deduplication.
Use AWS Glue ETL to create a custom job for processing and filtering duplicate records.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Glue ETL jobs provide a flexible scripting environment, using either Python shell or Apache Spark, for general-purpose data processing, but deduplicating records with fuzzy matching requires writing and maintaining substantial custom logic. This approach adds considerable coding complexity compared to the purpose-built, no-code AWS Glue FindMatches ML transform, violating the minimal code development constraint. Keyword tip: AWS Glue ETL is a general-purpose transformation tool; when fuzzy-match deduplication is the requirement, AWS Glue FindMatches is the preferred lower-overhead alternative within the same AWS Glue ecosystem.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of AWS services for data quality, record deduplication, and entity resolution in the Data Preparation domain.

It focuses on recognizing AWS Glue FindMatches as the managed, ML-powered transform for detecting duplicate and near-duplicate records with minimal custom code.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use AWS Glue FindMatches to automatically detect and group duplicate records in the dataset: This is the correct option. AWS Glue FindMatches is a machine learning transform designed to identify matching or duplicate records, including records that are not exact matches. It supports fuzzy matching across fields such as names, addresses, and phone numbers.

    Use Amazon EMR with Apache Spark to write a custom deduplication script using SparkSQL: Amazon EMR with Apache Spark can process large datasets, but this approach requires custom deduplication logic, cluster management, performance tuning, and ongoing script maintenance. It does not meet the requirement for minimal code and least operational overhead.

    Use SageMaker Data Wrangler to detect and group duplicate records in the dataset by leveraging its data preparation and transformation features: SageMaker Data Wrangler is useful for interactive data preparation, cleaning, transformation, and feature engineering. However, it is not the purpose-built service for ML-powered fuzzy matching or entity resolution across near-duplicate records.

    Use AWS Glue ETL to create a custom job for processing and filtering duplicate records: AWS Glue ETL can perform general-purpose data transformations, but fuzzy deduplication would require custom Python or Spark logic. This adds more development and maintenance effort than using AWS Glue FindMatches.

Why AWS Glue FindMatches fits this scenario.

AWS Glue FindMatches is the best fit when the dataset contains duplicate records that may not be exact matches.

In this scenario, records can include small variations, such as spelling differences, incomplete addresses, or inconsistent phone number formatting.

A simple exact-match deduplication job would not reliably identify those records as duplicates.

FindMatches uses machine learning to detect and group records that likely refer to the same entity.

It is especially useful for entity resolution problems involving contact names, addresses, and phone numbers.

It also integrates with Amazon S3 and the AWS Glue Data Catalog, making it suitable for data preparation workflows with low operational overhead.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Duplicate detection: Use AWS Glue FindMatches.

    Near-duplicate detection: Use fuzzy matching.

    Minimal code development: Avoid custom Spark or Python deduplication logic.

    Large dataset in Amazon S3: Use AWS Glue integration.

    Entity resolution: Group records that likely represent the same person, customer, vendor, or organization.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Near-duplicate records + fuzzy matching + names, addresses, or phone numbers + minimal code = AWS Glue FindMatches.

Remember:

    Fuzzy deduplication: Use AWS Glue FindMatches.

    Custom distributed processing: Use Amazon EMR only when custom Spark control is required.

    General ETL scripting: Use AWS Glue ETL, but it is higher overhead for fuzzy matching.

    Interactive feature preparation: Use SageMaker Data Wrangler.

    Entity resolution: Look for AWS Glue FindMatches.

Official AWS references.

    AWS Glue FindMatches.

Key exam terms: AWS Glue FindMatches, fuzzy matching, record deduplication, entity resolution, least operational overhead, minimal code development, Amazon S3, AWS Glue Data Catalog.

Keep practicing.

Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML)
Question 4Skipped

A media streaming company called StreamVault runs a data aggregation job every Saturday night to process viewership and engagement data from the past week.

The job is scheduled to run for approximately 2 hours and can tolerate interruptions without impacting the results.

The company plans to run this job consistently every weekend for the next 6 months. Which EC2 instance purchasing option will meet these requirements MOST cost-effectively?


Use EC2 Reserved Instances with a 6-month commitment to ensure lower costs and guaranteed availability.
Explanation
Incorrect. EC2 Reserved Instances offer discounted pricing for workloads that run continuously and predictably, but the minimum commitment term is 1 year, not 6 months. Purchasing Reserved Instances for a weekly 2-hour job over a 6-month period would result in substantial wasted spend on unused compute capacity outside that narrow usage window, making them far less cost-effective than Spot Instances for this access pattern. Keyword tip: Reserved Instances deliver value only for steady-state, long-running workloads with 1-year or 3-year commitments — short-duration or sub-annual workloads are poor fits.
Use EC2 Dedicated Hosts to run the job for full control over the infrastructure and long-term cost efficiency.
Explanation
Incorrect. EC2 Dedicated Hosts provide physical server isolation and are designed for workloads with strict compliance, software licensing, or regulatory requirements that mandate dedicated hardware. They carry a significant cost premium and offer no inherent benefit for a short-duration, interruptible batch job that has no hardware isolation or licensing constraints. Keyword tip: Dedicated Hosts are a compliance or BYOL (Bring Your Own License) solution, not a cost optimization tool for periodic batch workloads.
Use EC2 On-Demand Instances to run the job each weekend for 2 hours, paying only for the compute time consumed.
Explanation
Incorrect. EC2 On-Demand Instances provide guaranteed availability and are billed only for the time consumed, but they are significantly more expensive than Spot Instances for equivalent compute capacity. Since the workload explicitly tolerates interruptions, paying On-Demand prices is unnecessary and fails the cost-effectiveness requirement. Keyword tip: On-Demand pricing is appropriate when availability must be guaranteed and interruptions are not acceptable — the opposite constraint from this scenario.
Correct answer
Use EC2 Spot Instances to run the aggregation job, as they provide substantial cost savings and can handle interruptions.
Explanation
Correct. EC2 Spot Instances are the optimal choice for this scenario. They leverage unused EC2 capacity at up to 90% savings compared to On-Demand pricing. Because the job runs during off-peak hours (Saturday night) and explicitly tolerates interruptions, Spot Instances align perfectly with this use case — especially for a short-duration, infrequent batch workload where cost efficiency is the primary driver. Keyword tip: whenever a scenario states that a batch or aggregation workload 'can tolerate interruptions,' Spot Instances are the canonical cost optimization strategy to identify on the MLA-C01 exam.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of EC2 instance purchasing options for cost-optimized ML infrastructure.

It focuses on selecting EC2 Spot Instances when a workload is infrequent, short-running, fault-tolerant, and able to handle interruptions.

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use EC2 Spot Instances to run the aggregation job, as they provide substantial cost savings and can handle interruptions: This is the correct option. EC2 Spot Instances use spare EC2 capacity and can provide major cost savings compared with On-Demand pricing. They are a strong fit for batch jobs that can tolerate interruptions.

    Use EC2 On-Demand Instances to run the job each weekend for 2 hours, paying only for the compute time consumed: On-Demand Instances provide flexibility and do not require long-term commitment, but they are more expensive than Spot Instances. Since the job explicitly tolerates interruptions, On-Demand is not the most cost-effective choice.

    Use EC2 Reserved Instances with a 6-month commitment to ensure lower costs and guaranteed availability: This option is incorrect because Reserved Instances require a longer commitment period and are best suited for steady, predictable usage. A weekly 2-hour job would leave most of the reserved capacity unused.

    Use EC2 Dedicated Hosts to run the job for full control over the infrastructure and long-term cost efficiency: Dedicated Hosts are used when physical server isolation, licensing requirements, or strict compliance constraints are needed. They are not cost-effective for a short, interruptible batch aggregation job.

Why EC2 Spot Instances fit this scenario.

The workload is a batch aggregation job.

It runs infrequently.

It only runs for a short period.

It explicitly tolerates interruptions.

That combination points directly to EC2 Spot Instances.

Spot Instances are designed for workloads that can handle interruption and benefit from lower compute cost.

Batch processing, data aggregation, distributed analytics, image processing, and non-urgent ML jobs are common examples.

Because the job runs during an off-peak window and does not require uninterrupted execution, the company can use spare EC2 capacity at a lower price.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Short-duration workload: Avoid long-term capacity commitments.

    Infrequent execution: Do not reserve always-on compute.

    Interruption tolerance: Use Spot Instances.

    Cost optimization: Take advantage of spare EC2 capacity.

    Batch processing: Match the workload to a fault-tolerant compute option.

    No dedicated hardware requirement: Avoid Dedicated Hosts.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Batch job + can tolerate interruptions + cost optimization = EC2 Spot Instances.

Remember:

    Spot Instances: Best for interruptible, fault-tolerant, cost-sensitive workloads.

    On-Demand Instances: Best for flexible workloads that cannot tolerate interruption.

    Reserved Instances: Best for steady, predictable usage with long-term commitment.

    Dedicated Hosts: Best for compliance, licensing, or physical isolation requirements.

    Short weekly jobs: Usually do not justify Reserved Instances.

    Interruption tolerance: The strongest signal for Spot.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon EC2 instance purchasing options.

Key exam terms: EC2 Spot Instances, fault-tolerant batch job, interruption tolerance, cost optimization, On-Demand pricing, Reserved Instances minimum commitment, Dedicated Hosts compliance, weekend aggregation workload.

Question 5Skipped

You are a Data Scientist working for EduPath Learning, an education technology company that is developing a machine learning model to predict whether a student will complete a course enrollment based on their platform interaction behavior.

You need to evaluate the model's performance using different evaluation metrics to understand how well the model is predicting the positive class (i.e., students who will complete enrollment). The dataset is imbalanced, with a small percentage of students completing an enrollment event.

Given this context, you must decide on the most appropriate evaluation techniques to assess your model's effectiveness and identify potential areas for improvement. 

Which of the following evaluation techniques and metrics should you prioritize when assessing the performance of your model, considering the dataset's imbalance and the need for a comprehensive understanding of both false positives and false negatives? (Select TWO.)
Correct selection
Evaluate the model using the confusion matrix, which provides insights into true positives, false positives, true negatives, and false negatives, allowing you to calculate additional metrics such as precision, recall, and F1 score.
Explanation
Correct. The confusion matrix is the foundational diagnostic tool for evaluating classification models on imbalanced datasets. It explicitly enumerates true positives, false positives, true negatives, and false negatives, enabling the direct derivation of precision, recall, and F1 score. This level of granularity is critical for identifying whether the model is systematically biased toward the majority class — a key concern when a small fraction of EduPath Learning's users complete enrollment. Amazon SageMaker Autopilot and Amazon SageMaker Clarify both surface confusion matrix-based metrics in their model evaluation reports. Keyword tip: In MLA-C01 scenarios involving imbalanced classification and the explicit need to 'understand error types,' the confusion matrix is the primary diagnostic cornerstone from which all other classification metrics are derived.
Prioritize Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE) as the key metric, as it measures the average magnitude of the errors between predicted and actual values.
Explanation
Incorrect. RMSE (Root Mean Squared Error) is a regression metric used to quantify the magnitude of prediction errors for continuous output variables. The scenario at EduPath Learning describes a binary classification task (will a student complete enrollment or not?), for which classification metrics such as precision, recall, F1 score, and the confusion matrix are appropriate. Applying RMSE to a classification problem is a fundamental category error. Keyword tip: Whenever a question involves binary or multiclass classification and an answer option suggests RMSE or MAE, it is a deliberate distractor targeting confusion between regression and classification evaluation frameworks.
Correct selection
Use precision and recall to focus on the model's ability to correctly identify positive cases while minimizing false positives and false negatives.
Explanation
Correct. Precision and recall are essential metrics for imbalanced classification problems. Precision measures the proportion of true positive predictions among all positive predictions (minimizing false positives), while recall measures the proportion of actual positives that are correctly identified (minimizing false negatives). In a scenario where the positive class is rare — such as students completing enrollment at EduPath Learning — these metrics expose model weaknesses that accuracy conceals, directly quantifying the cost of each error type. Keyword tip: When you see 'imbalanced dataset' paired with 'false positives vs. false negatives trade-off' in an MLA-C01 scenario, precision and recall are the most targeted and actionable metrics to prioritize.
Use accuracy as the primary metric, as it measures the percentage of correct predictions out of all predictions made by the model.
Explanation
Incorrect. Accuracy is a misleading primary metric for imbalanced datasets. A naive model that always predicts the majority class (students who do not complete enrollment) can achieve very high accuracy while completely failing to identify the minority class (students who do enroll). This makes accuracy an unreliable indicator of model quality when class distribution is highly skewed. Keyword tip: Any MLA-C01 question mentioning 'imbalanced dataset' and proposing accuracy as the primary metric is a deliberate distractor — accuracy is only meaningful when class distributions are approximately balanced.
Utilize the AUC-ROC curve to evaluate the model's ability to distinguish between classes across various thresholds, particularly in the presence of class imbalance.
Explanation
Incorrect. Although the AUC-ROC curve is a valuable tool for evaluating classifiers across multiple decision thresholds and is indeed relevant in imbalanced settings, it does not provide direct, actionable insight into the specific error types (false positives vs. false negatives) at a chosen operating threshold. The question explicitly requires a comprehensive understanding of both false positives and false negatives, which the confusion matrix combined with precision and recall addresses more directly and granularly than AUC-ROC alone. Keyword tip: AUC-ROC is appropriate for threshold-agnostic, aggregate evaluation, but when an MLA-C01 question emphasizes 'understanding both false positives and false negatives' at a specific operating point, the confusion matrix and precision/recall are the more precisely targeted answers.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests understanding of classification model evaluation strategies under class imbalance.

It focuses on why aggregate metrics such as accuracy can be misleading when one class is much more frequent than the other.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use precision and recall to focus on the model's ability to correctly identify positive cases while minimizing false positives and false negatives: This is a correct option. Precision measures the proportion of predicted positive cases that are actually positive. Recall measures the proportion of actual positive cases that the model correctly identifies. These metrics are especially useful when the positive class is rare.

    Use accuracy as the primary metric, as it measures the percentage of correct predictions out of all predictions made by the model: Accuracy is a poor primary metric for imbalanced datasets. A model can achieve high accuracy by predicting only the majority class while failing to identify the minority class.

    Prioritize Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE) as the key metric, as it measures the average magnitude of the errors between predicted and actual values: RMSE is a regression metric. It is used when the target is a continuous numerical value. This scenario is a binary classification problem, so RMSE is not appropriate.

    Utilize the AUC-ROC curve to evaluate the model's ability to distinguish between classes across various thresholds, particularly in the presence of class imbalance: AUC-ROC can be useful for evaluating a classifier across multiple thresholds. However, it does not directly show the specific false positive and false negative counts at a chosen decision threshold. In this scenario, the question asks for direct visibility into both error types.

    Evaluate the model using the confusion matrix, which provides insights into true positives, false positives, true negatives, and false negatives, allowing you to calculate additional metrics such as precision, recall, and F1 score: This is a correct option. The confusion matrix is the foundational diagnostic tool for classification problems. It shows true positives, false positives, true negatives, and false negatives, which makes it possible to calculate precision, recall, and F1 score.

Why precision and recall with the confusion matrix fit this scenario.

In imbalanced classification problems, the minority class is often the class that matters most.

A model can look good on paper if it predicts the majority class correctly most of the time.

However, that same model may completely fail to identify the rare positive class.

That is why accuracy is not enough.

Precision helps evaluate false positives.

It answers this question:

    Of all records predicted as positive, how many were actually positive?

Recall helps evaluate false negatives.

It answers this question:

    Of all actual positive records, how many did the model correctly find?

The confusion matrix gives the full classification breakdown:

    True positives: Positive cases correctly predicted as positive.

    False positives: Negative cases incorrectly predicted as positive.

    True negatives: Negative cases correctly predicted as negative.

    False negatives: Positive cases incorrectly predicted as negative.

This combination is stronger than accuracy alone because it shows whether the model is actually detecting the minority class.

It is also more directly actionable than AUC-ROC when the scenario asks for visibility into specific error types.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Imbalanced dataset evaluation: Use precision, recall, and confusion matrix.

    False positive analysis: Use precision and confusion matrix.

    False negative analysis: Use recall and confusion matrix.

    Minority class visibility: Avoid relying on accuracy alone.

    Classification diagnostics: Use the confusion matrix to derive precision, recall, and F1 score.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Imbalanced dataset + binary classification + false positives and false negatives = precision, recall, and confusion matrix.

Remember:

    Precision: Focuses on false positives.

    Recall: Focuses on false negatives.

    F1 score: Balances precision and recall.

    Confusion matrix: Shows true positives, false positives, true negatives, and false negatives.

    Accuracy: Can be misleading when classes are imbalanced.

    RMSE: Use for regression, not classification.

    AUC-ROC: Useful as a threshold-agnostic complement, but not the most direct answer when the question asks about specific error types.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon SageMaker Autopilot metrics and validation.

    Binary classification.

Key exam terms: imbalanced dataset, binary classification, confusion matrix, precision, recall, F1 score, accuracy as distractor, RMSE category error, AUC-ROC threshold-agnostic, Amazon SageMaker Clarify, minority class evaluation.

Question 6Skipped

You are a data scientist working for AquaFlow Analytics, a water utility company that uses a machine learning model to predict infrastructure failure events and service disruption risks across its distribution network.

The model was trained on historical sensor readings, maintenance logs, and consumption patterns from the past five years, but after being deployed for several months, its accuracy has gradually decreased.

Upon investigation, you suspect that the underlying data distribution has changed due to aging infrastructure upgrades, seasonal demand shifts, and changes in regional water consumption behavior. This phenomenon is known as model drift, and you need to address it to ensure the model continues to perform well.

  Which of the following approaches would you combine for detecting and managing drift in your ML model? (Select TWO.)
Decrease the complexity of the model by removing features and layers, thereby turning it into a simpler model that can handle various types of data distributions.
Explanation
Incorrect. Reducing model complexity by removing features and layers introduces additional bias, making the model less capable of capturing the signal patterns required for accurate infrastructure failure predictions across AquaFlow Analytics's distribution network. A simpler model does not inherently generalize better across shifting distributions; it instead loses the expressive power needed to distinguish meaningful signal from noise in high-dimensional sensor and operational data. Simplification is a strategy for addressing overfitting during model development, not a solution for distributional drift in a deployed system. Keyword tip: answer options that propose reducing model complexity as a fix for production accuracy degradation are distractors — this addresses overfitting, not data distribution drift.
Deploy a secondary model trained on different data and compare its predictions with the original model to detect any significant differences, indicating potential drift.
Explanation
Incorrect. Comparing predictions from a secondary model trained on different data against the primary model's outputs is not a robust or scalable drift detection strategy. This approach requires provisioning, maintaining, and continuously updating a second production endpoint, adding significant operational overhead while providing no direct statistical evidence of distributional shift in the incoming feature data. Shadow deployment and A/B testing patterns do involve secondary models, but those serve the purpose of comparing model performance or safely rolling out a new model version — not detecting drift in input data distributions. Keyword tip: shadow deployment and A/B testing with secondary models are production rollout and performance comparison patterns in SageMaker; they are distractors when a question specifically asks about drift detection mechanisms.
Correct selection
Implement continuous monitoring of input data features and model predictions using statistical tests to detect shifts in data distribution or performance, triggering an alert when drift is detected.
Explanation
Correct. Implementing continuous monitoring of input data features and model predictions using statistical tests — such as the Population Stability Index, Kolmogorov-Smirnov test, or chi-squared test — is the standard mechanism for detecting data drift and concept drift before they cause significant accuracy degradation. Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor automates this process by comparing incoming feature distributions against a baseline captured at training time, and can trigger alerts or downstream retraining pipelines via Amazon CloudWatch alarms and Amazon EventBridge rules. This approach provides direct, statistically grounded evidence of distributional change without requiring a secondary deployed model. Keyword tip: when a scenario involves gradual accuracy degradation in production and asks about detection mechanisms, Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor combined with statistical baseline comparisons and Amazon CloudWatch integration is the primary AWS-native drift detection answer.
Increase the complexity of the model by adding more features and deeper layers, ensuring it can adapt to changing data distributions over time.
Explanation
Incorrect. Increasing model complexity by adding more features and deeper layers does not address the root cause of drift, which is a shift in the underlying data distribution rather than insufficient model capacity. In practice, a more complex model may overfit to noise in the most recently observed data or become more sensitive to minor distributional fluctuations, worsening generalization. Complexity tuning is a model development and architecture concern addressed during experimentation, not a drift remediation strategy in a deployed production system. Keyword tip: when an answer option proposes architectural changes such as adding layers or features as a response to degrading accuracy in production, recognize this as a distractor unrelated to drift detection or remediation.
Correct selection
Retrain the model on the most recent sensor and operational data to ensure it captures current infrastructure trends, and use model versioning to track performance improvements over time.
Explanation
Correct. Retraining the model on the most recent operational and sensor data directly addresses model drift by updating the model's learned parameters to reflect the current data distribution observed across AquaFlow Analytics's network. Combining this with model versioning — as supported by Amazon SageMaker Model Registry — allows teams to track performance improvements and roll back to previous versions if a retrained model underperforms. On MLA-C01, periodic or event-triggered retraining pipelines built with Amazon SageMaker Pipelines represent the standard MLOps remediation pattern once drift is confirmed. Keyword tip: when a scenario describes confirmed distributional shift requiring remediation, look for answer options that mention retraining on recent data combined with Amazon SageMaker Pipelines and Amazon SageMaker Model Registry.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to detect and remediate model drift in a production ML system.

It focuses on combining continuous statistical monitoring with retraining on recent data and model versioning.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Retrain the model on the most recent sensor and operational data to ensure it captures current infrastructure trends, and use model versioning to track performance improvements over time: This is a correct option. Retraining with recent data updates the model so it reflects current infrastructure conditions. SageMaker Model Registry can track model versions, approval status, and performance changes over time.

    Increase the complexity of the model by adding more features and deeper layers, ensuring it can adapt to changing data distributions over time: Increasing model complexity does not directly solve drift. Drift is caused by changes in data distributions or real-world relationships, not necessarily by insufficient model capacity. A more complex model may overfit and perform worse.

    Implement continuous monitoring of input data features and model predictions using statistical tests to detect shifts in data distribution or performance, triggering an alert when drift is detected: This is a correct option. Continuous monitoring helps detect drift before it causes severe production impact. SageMaker Model Monitor can compare live inference data against a baseline and publish alerts through services such as Amazon CloudWatch.

    Decrease the complexity of the model by removing features and layers, thereby turning it into a simpler model that can handle various types of data distributions: Simplifying the model is not a direct drift remediation strategy. It may reduce overfitting in development, but it does not solve the problem of production data or target relationships changing over time.

    Deploy a secondary model trained on different data and compare its predictions with the original model to detect any significant differences, indicating potential drift: A secondary model can be useful for shadow testing or rollout comparison, but it is not the most direct or scalable way to detect drift. Drift detection should be based on statistical monitoring of input distributions, model outputs, and performance metrics.

Why monitoring plus retraining fits this scenario.

The scenario describes a production model operating on time-varying real-world data.

In a utility infrastructure environment, sensor readings and operational patterns can change over time.

Equipment ages.

Maintenance practices change.

Weather patterns may affect infrastructure behavior.

New devices or calibration changes may alter sensor distributions.

These changes can make the original model less accurate even if the deployed model artifact has not changed.

The correct MLOps strategy has two parts.

First, the team needs continuous drift detection.

Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor can compare live inference traffic against a baseline created from training data.

It can detect changes in input feature distributions, prediction patterns, and model quality when ground truth is available.

Alerts can be sent through Amazon CloudWatch and used to trigger downstream workflows.

Second, the team needs a remediation path.

When drift is detected, the model should be retrained using recent sensor and operational data.

This retraining workflow can be orchestrated with Amazon SageMaker Pipelines.

The new model version can be registered and tracked in Amazon SageMaker Model Registry.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Drift detection: Use SageMaker Model Monitor.

    Statistical monitoring: Compare live data against a baseline.

    Alerting: Use CloudWatch alarms or EventBridge rules.

    Drift remediation: Retrain on recent production data.

    Workflow automation: Use SageMaker Pipelines.

    Model versioning: Use SageMaker Model Registry.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Production accuracy degradation + changing real-world data + drift detection and remediation = SageMaker Model Monitor plus retraining with SageMaker Pipelines.

Remember:

    Data drift: Input feature distribution changes.

    Model drift: Model performance degrades because the learned assumptions no longer match current conditions.

    SageMaker Model Monitor: Detects drift using statistical baselines and monitoring jobs.

    CloudWatch and EventBridge: Trigger alerts or automation.

    SageMaker Pipelines: Orchestrates retraining workflows.

    SageMaker Model Registry: Tracks model versions and deployment readiness.

    Changing model complexity: Not the primary fix for production drift.

Official AWS references.

    Automate model retraining with Amazon SageMaker Pipelines when drift is detected.

Question 7Skipped

A data scientist at a weather and climate research agency called AtmosMetrics is developing a machine learning model to predict the likelihood of equipment maintenance claims being fraudulent.

The agency has a strong commitment to fairness and wants to ensure that the model does not disproportionately affect any specific demographic group.

The team decides to use Amazon SageMaker Clarify to assess potential bias in the model. In particular, they are interested in understanding how the model's predictions differ across demographic groups when conditioned on relevant factors like income level, which could influence the likelihood of fraudulent claims. 

Given this scenario, which of the following BEST describes how Conditional Demographic Disparity (CDD) can be used to assess and mitigate bias in this model?
CDD assesses the proportion of correctly predicted outcomes for each demographic group, helping to ensure that the model is equally accurate across groups.
Explanation
Incorrect. This option describes accuracy parity or per-group accuracy metrics, which assess whether predictions are correct at equal rates across demographic groups. CDD does not evaluate prediction correctness; it measures disparities in positive prediction rates conditioned on a specific feature value. Confusing CDD with accuracy-based fairness metrics is a common error because both involve demographic group comparisons. Keyword tip: options mentioning 'correctly predicted outcomes' or 'equally accurate across groups' point to accuracy-based fairness metrics, not CDD.
CDD measures the difference in average predicted outcomes between demographic groups, helping to identify overall bias without considering other factors.
Explanation
Incorrect. This option describes a simpler, unconditional measure of demographic disparity — specifically the Demographic Disparity (DD) metric — which compares average predicted outcomes between groups without any conditioning on a covariate. CDD specifically adds the conditioning step on a relevant feature like income, making it more precise and capable of detecting bias hidden by confounding variables. Keyword tip: when an option explicitly states 'without considering other factors,' it signals an unconditional disparity metric such as DD, not the conditioned variant CDD.
Correct answer
CDD evaluates the disparity in positive prediction rates across demographic groups, conditioned on a specific feature like income, to detect bias that may not be apparent when only considering overall outcomes.
Explanation
Correct. Conditional Demographic Disparity (CDD) in Amazon SageMaker Clarify measures the difference in positive prediction rates between demographic groups while conditioning on a relevant feature such as income level. This conditioning step is critical because it reveals subtle biases that are masked when examining only aggregate prediction outcomes. By stratifying results on a conditioning variable, CDD provides a more nuanced and accurate view of fairness across groups given their specific circumstances. Keyword tip: when a question references SageMaker Clarify bias metrics, conditioning on a feature variable, and disparity in positive prediction rates simultaneously, CDD is the correct metric to select.
CDD focuses on the relationship between feature importance and demographic groups, highlighting whether certain features disproportionately influence predictions for specific groups.
Explanation
Incorrect. This option describes feature importance analysis, such as SHAP-based explainability methods available in Amazon SageMaker Clarify, which quantify how much each feature contributes to model predictions for individual examples or groups. CDD does not analyze feature importance or contribution magnitudes; it strictly measures prediction rate disparities conditioned on a feature value. Keyword tip: when an option references 'feature importance' and 'disproportionate influence on predictions,' it describes SHAP or similar explainability tools within SageMaker Clarify, not CDD.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon SageMaker deployment guardrails and safe model update strategies for production ML systems.

It focuses on using controlled rollout patterns such as blue/green deployment and canary releases when model updates require monitoring, traffic shifting, version control, and rollback capability.

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Utilize Amazon SageMaker's blue/green deployment strategy to shift traffic gradually from the old model to the new one, ensuring that you can monitor performance and quickly revert if needed: This is a correct option. Blue/green deployment creates a new fleet for the updated model while the existing fleet continues serving traffic. SageMaker can shift traffic gradually and use Amazon CloudWatch alarms during the baking period to trigger rollback if issues occur.

    Use Amazon SageMaker's built-in versioning to manage different versions of the model, and deploy the new version in a canary release by redirecting a small percentage of traffic to it initially: This is a correct option. Canary releases expose only a small percentage of production traffic to the new model version first. This reduces blast radius and allows the team to validate real-world behavior before a full rollout.

    Create a backup of the current model, deploy the new version, and if any issues arise, manually roll back by redeploying the previous model version: This option is not ideal for production ML systems. Manual rollback increases recovery time, introduces human error, and does not provide the automated safeguards expected in safety-critical environments.

    Deploy the new model version alongside the current one, and use Amazon SageMaker's multi-model endpoint to serve both models simultaneously, splitting traffic between them: Multi-model endpoints are designed to host multiple models on shared infrastructure for cost efficiency. They are not the correct traffic-shifting or rollback mechanism for controlled deployment of a new version of the same model.

    Deploy the new model version immediately and redirect 100% of traffic to it, assuming it has been thoroughly tested and will not require a rollback: This option is too risky. Even well-tested models can fail under production traffic. In high-stakes environments, traffic should be shifted gradually with monitoring and rollback capability.

Why blue/green deployment and canary release fit this scenario.

The scenario involves a production model update in a high-stakes environment.

That means the deployment strategy must minimize risk.

A new model version should not receive all production traffic immediately.

Instead, the team should use a controlled rollout strategy.

SageMaker blue/green deployment is designed for this.

It keeps the old model fleet available while a new fleet is created for the updated model.

Traffic can then be shifted gradually from the blue fleet to the green fleet.

During the baking period, CloudWatch alarms can monitor operational or model-related metrics.

If an alarm is triggered, SageMaker can roll traffic back to the previous fleet.

A canary release is also appropriate.

In a canary release, only a small percentage of traffic is initially routed to the new model version.

This allows the team to validate behavior under real production conditions while limiting the impact of any failure.

SageMaker model versioning and registry capabilities also support governance, approval, traceability, and rollback planning.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Safe production deployment: Use controlled traffic shifting.

    Rollback readiness: Keep the previous version available.

    Automated protection: Use CloudWatch alarms and SageMaker deployment guardrails.

    Limited blast radius: Use canary traffic exposure.

    Auditability: Track model versions and approval state.

    High-stakes workload protection: Avoid immediate 100% traffic cutover.

Exam tip.

The key exam signals are:

Gradual traffic shift + baking period + CloudWatch alarms + automated rollback = SageMaker blue/green deployment.

Small initial traffic percentage + version control + production validation = canary release.

Remember:

    Blue/green deployment: Runs old and new fleets and shifts traffic safely.

    Canary release: Sends a small percentage of traffic to the new version first.

    CloudWatch alarms: Used to detect issues during deployment.

    Automated rollback: Reduces recovery time and operational risk.

    Model Registry: Supports versioning, approval, and governance.

    Multi-model endpoint: Cost-efficient hosting for multiple models, not a deployment rollback strategy.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon SageMaker blue/green deployment guardrails.

    Amazon SageMaker rolling deployment guardrails.

    Amazon SageMaker multi-model endpoints.

Question 8Skipped

A water utility company, AquaFlow Systems, trained an ML model on Amazon SageMaker to detect equipment defects from surveillance images captured at pumping stations and treatment facilities.

The training dataset was created using SageMaker Data Wrangler, which included images of defective and non-defective equipment components.

During training and validation, the model achieved high accuracy; however, in production, the model's performance is degraded due to differences in lighting conditions and image quality across various facility locations.

The ML engineer needs to improve the model's accuracy to handle variations in image quality with the LEAST amount of time and effort. What do you recommend?


Deploy a preprocessing pipeline in Amazon SageMaker to filter out low-quality images before sending them to the model for inference.
Explanation
Incorrect. Deploying a preprocessing pipeline to filter out low-quality images at inference time would reduce the volume of images the model processes but would not improve the model's underlying ability to handle image quality variation across AquaFlow Systems' facilities. Filtering could discard images containing real defect events, directly reducing recall and overall model accuracy in a safety-critical monitoring context. Furthermore, this approach addresses only inference-time behavior and does nothing to make the model itself more robust to the real-world data distribution. Keyword tip: filtering inputs at inference to avoid model weaknesses is a workaround, not a root-cause solution — the correct MLOps pattern is to improve training data distribution to reflect real-world conditions.
Use SageMaker Data Wrangler's outlier detection transform to remove outlier images from the dataset and improve the model's performance.
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Data Wrangler's outlier detection transform is designed for identifying and removing statistically anomalous data points in tabular or numerical datasets, not for addressing image quality variation caused by environmental factors such as lighting differences or camera inconsistencies. Applying this transform to an image dataset could inadvertently remove valuable training examples that happen to look statistically unusual, further degrading model generalization rather than improving it. Keyword tip: outlier detection transforms in SageMaker Data Wrangler apply to numerical and tabular data, not to image quality variation — this is a common distractor in MLA-C01 computer vision scenarios involving data preprocessing.
Fine-tune the model by manually collecting additional data from various facility cameras and retrain it with the new dataset.
Explanation
Incorrect. Manually collecting additional data from multiple cameras across facility locations and retraining the model is a valid long-term strategy, but it is the most time-intensive and resource-demanding option available. This approach requires significant human effort for data collection, labeling, and pipeline reconfiguration, which directly contradicts the requirement of least time and effort. Keyword tip: whenever a question asks for the lowest-effort or fastest solution to a data quality problem, options requiring manual data collection and full retraining pipeline reconstruction are strong distractors to eliminate first.
Correct answer
Use SageMaker Data Wrangler's corrupt image transform to preprocess the training data by simulating variations in image quality for robust model training.
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Data Wrangler's corrupt image transform is purpose-built to simulate real-world image imperfections such as noise, blurriness, and resolution degradation during the preprocessing stage. Applying this transformation augments the existing training dataset with synthetically degraded images, enabling the model to learn robust representations that generalize across varying lighting conditions and image quality levels encountered in production at AquaFlow Systems' distributed facilities. This approach requires minimal time and effort because it integrates directly into the existing SageMaker Data Wrangler workflow without requiring manual data collection, custom augmentation scripts, or a complete retraining pipeline rebuilt from scratch. Keyword tip: when an MLA-C01 scenario involves image quality variance causing production degradation and asks for the least-effort solution, the corrupt image transform in SageMaker Data Wrangler is the targeted answer.

This helps augment the training dataset so the model learns to handle production image variations more effectively.

Question 9Skipped

An online gaming company, PixelForge Games, is building a match session duration prediction model on AWS to estimate the number of minutes a player will remain in an active game session. The dataset includes features such as player skill rating, selected game mode, historical session lengths, and in-game event frequency. T

he model outputs a continuous numerical value representing the estimated session duration in minutes.


The ML engineer needs to evaluate the model's performance to determine how accurately it predicts session durations. Which metric should the ML engineer use to evaluate the model's performance?



Area Under the ROC Curve (AUC-ROC)
Explanation
Incorrect. AUC-ROC measures the ability of a binary classification model to discriminate between positive and negative classes by evaluating the trade-off between true positive rate and false positive rate across all classification thresholds. It is entirely inapplicable to regression problems where the target variable is a continuous numerical value such as estimated session duration in minutes. Applying AUC-ROC to a regression output would produce a meaningless result because there are no discrete class boundaries to evaluate. Keyword tip: whenever AUC-ROC appears as an option for a scenario involving a continuous numeric output target, eliminate it immediately — AUC-ROC is strictly a binary classification metric.
Correct answer
Mean Absolute Error (MAE)
Explanation
Correct. Mean Absolute Error (MAE) is the standard evaluation metric for regression tasks where the model produces continuous numerical predictions. MAE computes the average of the absolute differences between predicted and actual values, yielding an interpretable error expressed in the same units as the target variable — minutes, in this case — which makes it straightforward for stakeholders to understand. Unlike Mean Squared Error (MSE) or Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE), MAE does not square the residuals, making it less sensitive to outliers and more directly interpretable as an average deviation. Amazon SageMaker Autopilot supports MAE as a primary objective metric for regression problems, and it can also be specified as a custom evaluation metric in manual SageMaker training job configurations. Keyword tip: when the ML task is regression with a continuous numeric target and interpretability in original units combined with robustness to outliers is required, MAE is the canonical choice on the MLA-C01 exam.
Precision
Explanation
Incorrect. Precision measures the proportion of true positive predictions among all positive predictions (TP / (TP + FP)) and is a metric defined exclusively for classification tasks involving discrete class labels. It has no meaningful mathematical interpretation when applied to regression problems that produce continuous output values such as session duration. There is no concept of a 'true positive' or 'false positive' when the model output is a real-valued number on a continuous scale. Keyword tip: Precision, Recall, and F1-Score are classification metrics; scenarios involving continuous numeric targets require regression metrics such as MAE, MSE, or RMSE.
Accuracy
Explanation
Incorrect. Accuracy is defined as the ratio of correctly classified instances to the total number of instances and is meaningful only for classification problems with discrete class labels. It is undefined and inapplicable for regression tasks because regression outputs are continuous real-valued numbers rather than class assignments, and there is no natural notion of a prediction being exactly 'correct' or 'incorrect' on a continuous scale. Attempting to apply accuracy to a session duration prediction model would require arbitrarily discretizing the output, which is not a standard ML evaluation practice. Keyword tip: if the model output is a continuous number rather than a discrete class label, accuracy cannot be used as an evaluation metric — look instead for MAE, MSE, or RMSE in the answer options.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to select the correct model evaluation metric based on the type of ML problem.

It focuses on recognizing that a model predicting a continuous numerical value is a regression model, not a classification model.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Area Under the ROC Curve (AUC-ROC): AUC-ROC is used for binary classification. It evaluates how well a model separates positive and negative classes across different thresholds. It is not appropriate for regression because regression outputs continuous numerical values, not discrete classes.

    Precision: Precision is also a classification metric. It measures how many predicted positive cases are actually positive. It depends on concepts such as true positives and false positives, which do not apply to continuous numerical predictions.

    Mean Absolute Error (MAE): This is the correct option. Mean Absolute Error (MAE) is a regression metric. It measures the average absolute difference between predicted values and actual values. In this scenario, the error is expressed in the same unit as the target variable, such as minutes.

    Accuracy: Accuracy is a classification metric. It measures the proportion of correct class predictions. It is not meaningful for regression because continuous predictions are not simply correct or incorrect class labels.

Why Mean Absolute Error (MAE) fits this scenario.

The model in this scenario predicts a continuous numerical value.

The output is not a class such as high-risk or low-risk.

The output is a number, such as the predicted number of minutes a player will remain in a game session.

That makes this a regression problem.

For regression, the evaluation metric should measure how far the predicted value is from the actual value.

Mean Absolute Error (MAE) does this by calculating the average absolute difference between predictions and actual outcomes.

For example, if the target is session duration in minutes, MAE also reports error in minutes.

That makes it easy to interpret.

A lower MAE means the model's predictions are closer to the actual session durations.

MAE is also less sensitive to large outliers than metrics that square the error, such as Mean Squared Error (MSE).

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Continuous numeric prediction: Use a regression metric.

    Interpretable error: Use MAE because it stays in the original unit.

    Session duration prediction: Evaluate average prediction error in minutes.

    Classification metrics eliminated: Do not use AUC-ROC, precision, or accuracy.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Continuous numeric target + prediction error magnitude = regression metric such as MAE.

Remember:

    MAE: Regression metric; average absolute error in the original unit.

    MSE: Regression metric; squares errors and penalizes larger mistakes more strongly.

    RMSE: Regression metric; square root of MSE, also in the target unit.

    AUC-ROC: Binary classification metric.

    Precision: Classification metric focused on false positives.

    Accuracy: Classification metric based on correct class predictions.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon SageMaker Autopilot metrics and validation.

Key exam terms: regression metric, continuous numeric target, Mean Absolute Error (MAE), AUC-ROC inapplicable to regression, classification vs. regression evaluation, SageMaker Autopilot objective metric, MAE vs. MSE vs. RMSE, model evaluation metric selection.
