Question 28Skipped

An education technology company, LearnPath Analytics, has deployed a machine learning model using Amazon SageMaker to predict whether student assessment responses indicate academic dishonesty.

To ensure model performance remains consistent, the company configured Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor to track deviations in model accuracy over time. The model's baseline accuracy was recorded during its initial deployment.

However, after several months of operation, the model's accuracy drops significantly despite no changes being made to the model itself. 

What could be the reason for the reduced model accuracy?
Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor miscalculated the accuracy metric due to a configuration error
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor is a robust, well-tested managed service that accurately captures and evaluates monitoring metrics when correctly configured. If the monitoring job was functioning properly at baseline creation and no subsequent configuration changes were made, a sustained and significant accuracy decline is far more likely caused by a real-world data phenomenon such as concept drift than by a miscalculation within the monitoring service itself. Keyword tip: attributing a sustained performance decline to a correctly functioning AWS managed service is a common distractor pattern — MLA-C01 questions favor data or model drift as the root cause over service malfunction.
The model's hyperparameters have automatically adjusted during inference, causing a decline in predictive accuracy
Explanation
Incorrect. Hyperparameters are fixed at training time and are embedded in the model artifact; they are not dynamic values that adjust automatically during inference or production serving. Amazon SageMaker endpoints serve a static model, and no automatic hyperparameter adjustment occurs post-deployment unless the model is explicitly retrained and redeployed with new parameters. Amazon SageMaker Automatic Model Tuning applies only during training jobs, not during live inference. Keyword tip: 'hyperparameters adjust during inference' is always incorrect — hyperparameters are immutable in a deployed SageMaker endpoint.
The deployed model's architecture degraded over time, reducing its predictive performance
Explanation
Incorrect. Machine learning model architectures — whether neural networks, gradient-boosted trees, or linear models — do not degrade or deteriorate on their own over time. The model artifact stored in Amazon S3 and served by a SageMaker endpoint remains entirely static unless explicitly retrained and redeployed. Reduced accuracy in production is attributable to data-related phenomena such as data drift or concept drift, not to any physical or logical degradation of the model structure or serving infrastructure. Keyword tip: 'model architecture degrades over time' is a classic distractor — deployed model artifacts are immutable and do not self-modify.
Correct answer
Concept drift occurred in the underlying student data used for predictions, changing the relationship between input features and the target variable over time
Explanation
Correct. Concept drift occurs when the statistical relationship between input features and the target variable — P(Y|X) — changes over time, even if the input data distribution may appear superficially similar. In an academic dishonesty detection scenario, student behavior patterns evolve continuously: assessment formats change, new collaboration tools emerge, and student writing styles shift with generational cohorts. The model, trained on historical patterns, becomes misaligned with real-world behavior, leading to a measurable drop in accuracy without any modification to the model artifact itself. Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor can detect this type of drift through model quality monitoring, and the recommended MLOps mitigation is to trigger automated retraining via Amazon SageMaker Pipelines when drift is detected. Keyword tip: 'accuracy drop with no model changes' combined with 'evolving behavioral patterns over months' is the canonical signal for concept drift in MLA-C01 questions.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests understanding of concept drift in production ML systems.

It focuses on recognizing when the relationship between input features and the target variable changes over time, causing model accuracy to degrade even though the deployed model artifact has not changed.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Concept drift occurred in the underlying student data used for predictions, changing the relationship between input features and the target variable over time: This is the correct option. Concept drift occurs when the relationship between features and the target variable changes over time. In other words, the model learned one pattern during training, but the real-world relationship between inputs and outcomes has shifted in production.

    The deployed model's architecture degraded over time, reducing its predictive performance: Model architectures do not degrade by themselves over time. A deployed model artifact remains static unless it is explicitly retrained and redeployed. A decline in accuracy is usually caused by changes in data patterns, target behavior, or real-world conditions, not by the model structure physically or logically deteriorating.

    Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor miscalculated the accuracy metric due to a configuration error: A configuration issue is possible in real systems, but it is not the best explanation when the scenario describes a sustained decline after stable production behavior. If monitoring was correctly configured and the metric drop is consistent, the likely cause is a real ML drift phenomenon.

    The model's hyperparameters have automatically adjusted during inference, causing a decline in predictive accuracy: Hyperparameters are fixed during training. They do not automatically change during inference. SageMaker Automatic Model Tuning applies during training jobs, not while a deployed endpoint is serving predictions.

Why concept drift fits this scenario.

Concept drift occurs when the relationship between the input features and the target variable changes over time.

This is often described as a change in P(Y|X).

The input data may still look similar on the surface, but the meaning of those inputs has changed in relation to the outcome.

In an academic dishonesty detection scenario, student behavior can evolve over time.

Assessment formats may change.

Collaboration tools may change.

Writing styles may change.

New cheating patterns may emerge.

A model trained on historical behavior can become less accurate when those real-world patterns shift.

The model itself has not changed.

The endpoint has not automatically changed its hyperparameters.

The architecture has not degraded.

Instead, the learned decision boundary has become outdated because the production relationship between features and labels has changed.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Accuracy decline: The model performs worse over time.

    No model artifact change: The deployed model remains static.

    Behavioral pattern change: The real-world relationship between inputs and outcomes changes.

    Production monitoring: Use SageMaker Model Monitor to detect model quality degradation.

    MLOps response: Trigger retraining through SageMaker Pipelines when drift thresholds are breached.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Accuracy drops over time + no model changes + evolving real-world behavior = concept drift.

Remember:

    Concept drift: The relationship between X and Y changes.

    Data drift: The input feature distribution changes.

    Model quality drift: Performance metrics degrade when ground truth is available.

    Model artifact: Static unless retrained and redeployed.

    Hyperparameters: Fixed after training, not automatically adjusted during inference.

    Automated remediation: Use SageMaker Model Monitor with CloudWatch, EventBridge, and SageMaker Pipelines for retraining workflows.

Official AWS references.

    Automate model retraining with Amazon SageMaker Pipelines when drift is detected.

Key exam terms: concept drift, Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor, model quality monitoring, P(Y|X) distribution shift, accuracy degradation, no model changes, Amazon SageMaker Pipelines, automated retraining.

Question 29Skipped

A media streaming company, StreamVault Media, is deploying an ML model to predict content recommendation failure rates using Amazon SageMaker.


The company's ML engineer is setting up a CI/CD pipeline using AWS CodePipeline to automate the model retraining and deployment process.


The pipeline must automatically trigger when new training data is uploaded to an Amazon S3 bucket. The goal is to retrain the model and deploy it for real-time inference. Given this context, consider the following steps: 


1. The pipeline deploys the model version in SageMaker Model Monitor for real-time inferences.

2. A new data upload triggers the pipeline via an Amazon S3 event notification.

3. The pipeline deploys the retrained model to a SageMaker endpoint for real-time predictions.

4. Amazon SageMaker retrains the model using updated data stored in the S3 bucket.

5. An S3 Lifecycle rule attempts to start the pipeline when fresh data arrives. 


Which three steps should be selected and ordered correctly to configure the pipeline? 



2, 4, 1
Explanation
Incorrect. This sequence correctly uses an S3 event notification (step 2) to trigger the pipeline and Amazon SageMaker (step 4) to perform retraining, but then incorrectly specifies SageMaker Model Monitor (step 1) as the deployment target for real-time inference. SageMaker Model Monitor does not host or serve models; it is a monitoring layer that attaches to an already-deployed SageMaker endpoint to track data quality, model quality, bias drift, and feature attribution drift over time. The final step in a real-time deployment workflow must be creating or updating a SageMaker endpoint (step 3), not enabling a monitoring schedule. Keyword tip: If an answer option deploys to 'Model Monitor' for inference, eliminate it — Model Monitor monitors endpoints, it does not replace them as a serving target.
5, 4, 1
Explanation
Incorrect. This sequence incorrectly uses an S3 Lifecycle rule (step 5) as the pipeline trigger and attempts to deploy the model to SageMaker Model Monitor (step 1) for real-time inference. S3 Lifecycle rules are designed exclusively to transition objects between storage classes or expire them based on age or size-based policies — they cannot invoke AWS CodePipeline or initiate any event-driven workflow. Additionally, SageMaker Model Monitor is a post-deployment observability service used to detect data drift and model quality degradation on an already-running endpoint, not a deployment target for serving real-time predictions. Keyword tip: When you see 'S3 Lifecycle rule' as a pipeline trigger candidate, eliminate it immediately — Lifecycle rules govern object storage management only, never event-driven ML workflow invocation.
Correct answer
2, 4, 3
Explanation
Correct. This is the correct sequence for an event-driven MLOps retraining and deployment workflow. Step 2: Amazon S3 event notifications can be configured to automatically trigger AWS CodePipeline when new training data is uploaded to a bucket, enabling fully automated, hands-off pipeline execution. Step 4: After the pipeline is triggered, Amazon SageMaker retrains the model using the updated data in S3, ensuring the model reflects the most current patterns in the streaming data. Step 3: Once retraining is complete, the CI/CD pipeline deploys the updated model to a SageMaker endpoint, which hosts the model and serves real-time inference requests. Keyword tip: The canonical event-driven MLOps pattern on AWS is S3 event notification → CodePipeline trigger → SageMaker training job → SageMaker real-time endpoint deployment.
5, 4, 3
Explanation
Incorrect. This sequence incorrectly relies on an S3 Lifecycle rule (step 5) to initiate the pipeline, even though it correctly concludes with a SageMaker endpoint deployment (step 3). S3 Lifecycle rules manage object transitions (for example, moving objects to Amazon S3 Glacier) and expiration policies based on predefined schedules or object age — they are not capable of invoking AWS CodePipeline, AWS Lambda, or any event-driven workflow upon object upload. To trigger a pipeline automatically when new training data arrives, Amazon S3 event notifications must be used, either routed through Amazon EventBridge or configured directly as a CodePipeline source action. Keyword tip: Distinguish S3 event notifications (upload-triggered, event-driven) from S3 Lifecycle rules (time-based or size-based storage policy management) when evaluating pipeline trigger mechanisms.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of event-driven MLOps pipeline design using AWS CodePipeline, Amazon S3, and Amazon SageMaker.

It focuses on correctly sequencing an automated trigger, a model retraining step, and a real-time model deployment step.

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    5, 4, 1: This sequence is incorrect. S3 Lifecycle rules manage storage transitions and object expiration. They do not trigger CodePipeline when new data arrives. Also, SageMaker Model Monitor is not a model hosting service. It monitors deployed endpoints but does not serve real-time inference.

    2, 4, 3: This is the correct sequence. Amazon S3 event notifications can trigger the pipeline when new training data is uploaded. Amazon SageMaker retrains the model using the updated data. The updated model is then deployed to a SageMaker real-time endpoint for inference.

    2, 4, 1: This sequence starts correctly with an S3 event notification and SageMaker retraining, but the final step is wrong. SageMaker Model Monitor is used after deployment to monitor data quality, model quality, bias drift, or feature attribution drift. It is not the deployment target for real-time inference.

    5, 4, 3: This sequence ends correctly with a SageMaker endpoint deployment, but it starts with the wrong trigger. S3 Lifecycle rules cannot initiate event-driven ML pipelines. New data arrival should be detected with S3 event notifications or an event-based integration.

Why 2, 4, 3 fits this scenario.

The scenario requires an automated retraining and deployment workflow.

The pipeline must start when new training data arrives.

That points to Amazon S3 event notifications.

When new data is uploaded to the S3 bucket, the event can trigger AWS CodePipeline.

After the pipeline starts, the next step is model retraining.

That is handled by an Amazon SageMaker training job, which uses the updated training data from Amazon S3.

Once training completes, the newly trained model must be deployed for real-time inference.

That means the final target should be a SageMaker real-time endpoint.

This endpoint hosts the model and serves synchronous prediction requests.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    New data arrival trigger: Use S3 event notifications.

    Automated pipeline execution: Trigger AWS CodePipeline.

    Model retraining: Run a SageMaker training job.

    Real-time inference: Deploy to a SageMaker endpoint.

    Managed deployment workflow: Use CI/CD orchestration instead of manual updates.

    Avoid service-boundary mistakes: Do not use S3 Lifecycle rules as triggers or Model Monitor as a serving endpoint.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

New training data in S3 + automated retraining + real-time inference deployment = S3 event notification → CodePipeline → SageMaker training job → SageMaker endpoint.

Remember:

    S3 event notification: Detects object creation and can trigger downstream workflows.

    S3 Lifecycle rule: Moves or expires objects; it does not trigger CI/CD pipelines.

    AWS CodePipeline: Orchestrates the CI/CD workflow.

    SageMaker training job: Retrains the model using updated data.

    SageMaker endpoint: Hosts the model for real-time inference.

    SageMaker Model Monitor: Observes deployed endpoints; it does not deploy or serve models.

Official AWS references.

    Create a pipeline in AWS CodePipeline.

Question 30Skipped

An ML engineer at PeakPlay Analytics, a sports analytics company, is training a time series forecasting model using a recurrent neural network (RNN) to predict weekly in-stadium merchandise demand across multiple venues. The model is trained using stochastic gradient descent (SGD) as the optimizer.

During training, the engineer notices the following:  The training loss and validation loss remain high. The loss values oscillate, decreasing for a few epochs and then increasing again before repeating the cycle. 

The ML engineer needs to resolve this issue to stabilize the training process and improve model performance. What should the ML engineer do to improve the training process?
Increase the number of training epochs to give the model more time to learn the patterns in the data.
Explanation
Incorrect. Increasing the number of training epochs extends the duration of training but does not address the root cause of the oscillation. If the learning rate remains too high, the optimizer will continue to overshoot the loss minimum across every additional epoch without converging, making more epochs counterproductive in this scenario. Adding epochs is only beneficial when the loss is still decreasing at the end of training and the model simply needs more iterations to fit the data. Keyword tip: adding epochs helps with underfitting caused by insufficient training iterations, not with instability caused by a misconfigured learning rate.
Correct answer
Reduce the learning rate to allow the gradient updates to converge more smoothly and prevent oscillations in the loss values.
Explanation
Correct. An oscillating loss pattern — where the loss alternately decreases and increases across epochs without converging — is a classic symptom of a learning rate that is too high. When the learning rate is excessively large, gradient updates overshoot the optimal parameter values, causing the optimizer to repeatedly jump over the minimum rather than settling into it. Reducing the learning rate produces smaller, more incremental weight updates, which allows the model to converge more smoothly and stabilize the training process. Keyword tip: whenever you see oscillating or diverging loss paired with SGD or any gradient-based optimizer, a learning rate that is too high is the primary suspect — reducing it is the first corrective action to consider.
Increase the learning rate to allow the gradient updates to converge more smoothly and prevent oscillations in the loss values.
Explanation
Incorrect. Increasing the learning rate is the opposite of what is needed and would aggravate the instability. A higher learning rate produces larger gradient steps, making the optimizer overshoot the optimal solution even more aggressively and potentially causing the loss to diverge entirely rather than converge. Keyword tip: increasing the learning rate is appropriate only when training is progressing too slowly — that is, when loss is decreasing very gradually over many epochs — not when loss is already oscillating, which calls for a decrease.
Apply dropout regularization to the RNN layers to improve generalization and reduce oscillations in the loss.
Explanation
Incorrect. Dropout regularization is a technique designed to address overfitting by randomly deactivating neurons during training, forcing the network to learn more robust representations. The scenario describes training instability characterized by oscillating loss values at a persistently high level — a symptom of an optimization problem caused by an excessive learning rate, not a generalization problem. Applying dropout would not cure the root cause and may further destabilize an already unstable training run. Keyword tip: dropout is the correct remedy when training loss is low but validation loss is high (an overfitting gap is present), not when both losses oscillate at persistently high values.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests understanding of gradient-based optimization dynamics in neural network training.

It focuses on recognizing that an oscillating or unstable loss curve is commonly caused by a learning rate that is too high.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Reduce the learning rate to allow the gradient updates to converge more smoothly and prevent oscillations in the loss values: This is the correct option. When the learning rate is too high, gradient updates can overshoot the loss minimum. Reducing the learning rate creates smaller, more stable updates and helps the optimizer converge.

    Apply dropout regularization to the RNN layers to improve generalization and reduce oscillations in the loss: Dropout is used to reduce overfitting and improve generalization. It does not directly fix optimization instability caused by an excessively high learning rate. The scenario describes unstable training loss, not a generalization gap.

    Increase the learning rate to allow the gradient updates to converge more smoothly and prevent oscillations in the loss values: Increasing the learning rate would make the problem worse. Larger steps make the optimizer more likely to overshoot the minimum and can cause the loss to oscillate or diverge.

    Increase the number of training epochs to give the model more time to learn the patterns in the data: More epochs do not fix an unstable optimization process. If the learning rate is too high, the model will continue to overshoot the minimum across additional epochs instead of converging.

Why reducing the learning rate fits this scenario.

The scenario describes an oscillating loss pattern.

That means the loss repeatedly goes up and down instead of steadily decreasing toward convergence.

This is a classic sign that the optimizer is taking steps that are too large.

In gradient-based optimization, the learning rate controls the size of each parameter update.

When the learning rate is too high, the model may jump past the best parameter values instead of moving gradually toward them.

This causes the loss to oscillate around the minimum or even diverge.

Reducing the learning rate makes each update smaller and more controlled.

That gives the optimizer a better chance to move toward a stable minimum over successive epochs.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Oscillating loss: Reduce the learning rate.

    Training instability: Adjust the optimization step size.

    Gradient overshoot: Use smaller updates.

    Convergence problem: Fix the optimizer behavior before adding regularization.

    RNN training: Learning rate stability is especially important for recurrent models.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Oscillating or diverging loss + gradient-based optimizer = learning rate is too high, reduce it.

Remember:

    High learning rate: Can cause overshooting, oscillation, or divergence.

    Lower learning rate: Produces smoother, more stable convergence.

    Dropout: Helps with overfitting, not learning-rate overshoot.

    More epochs: Helps only when training is still improving normally.

    Optimization problem: Look at the learning rate.

    Generalization problem: Look at dropout, L1/L2 regularization, or early stopping.

Official AWS references.

    Training parameters.

Key exam terms: learning rate, oscillating loss, stochastic gradient descent (SGD), RNN, gradient overshoot, training instability, dropout regularization, optimization vs. generalization.

Question 31Skipped

A pharmaceutical research organization, PharmaData Insights, stores historical clinical trial observation data in.csv files in Amazon S3. The data is partially populated, lacks column labels, and contains missing values. The organization needs to prepare and structure this data so it can be used effectively for training ML models. Given this context, consider the following five steps:


1. Use AWS Glue crawlers to infer the schemas and available columns.

2. Use Amazon EMR with Apache Spark for data cleaning and feature engineering.

3. Store the resulting data back in Amazon S3.

4. Use Amazon Redshift Spectrum to infer the schemas and available columns.

5. Use AWS Glue DataBrew for data cleaning and feature engineering.


What is the correct order in which three of these steps should be selected to achieve this task efficiently?


Correct answer
1, 5, 3
Explanation
Correct. This sequence represents the most efficient and fully managed approach for preparing unstructured.csv clinical trial data in Amazon S3 for ML model training. First, AWS Glue Crawlers automatically analyze the S3 files and infer their schema, populating the AWS Glue Data Catalog with table definitions — a prerequisite for any downstream transformation. Next, AWS Glue DataBrew — a visual, no-code/low-code data preparation service — is used to clean the data by handling missing values, standardizing column formats, and engineering features without requiring custom distributed processing code. Finally, the cleaned and structured data is written back to Amazon S3, where it is readily accessible for downstream ML training jobs. This pipeline minimizes operational overhead by leveraging fully managed, purpose-built AWS services at every stage. Keyword tip: The combination of 'schema inference from S3,' 'visual data cleaning with low operational overhead,' and 'ML training readiness' maps directly to the sequence AWS Glue Crawlers → AWS Glue DataBrew → Amazon S3.
5, 1, 3
Explanation
Incorrect. Data cleaning and feature engineering with AWS Glue DataBrew cannot be performed effectively before the schema of the underlying data has been inferred. Without a defined schema registered in the AWS Glue Data Catalog, DataBrew cannot correctly interpret column types, identify missing or malformed values, or apply transformation recipes to the dataset. Schema discovery via AWS Glue Crawlers is a hard prerequisite that must precede the data transformation step. Keyword tip: In any well-architected ML data preparation pipeline, schema discovery is always a prerequisite for data transformation — performing AWS Glue DataBrew steps before running AWS Glue Crawlers violates this fundamental sequence dependency.
4, 5, 3
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Redshift Spectrum enables querying data stored in Amazon S3 using Redshift's SQL engine, but it does not automatically infer schemas from raw files. Schema inference — the process of analyzing file contents and registering table definitions — is a native capability of AWS Glue Crawlers, which populate the AWS Glue Data Catalog. Using Redshift Spectrum as a first step would require the schema to already exist, making it unsuitable as a schema discovery mechanism in an ML data preparation pipeline. Keyword tip: When the scenario involves automatically inferring schemas from raw.csv files in Amazon S3, associate that capability exclusively with AWS Glue Crawlers, not Amazon Redshift Spectrum.
1, 2, 3
Explanation
Incorrect. Although Amazon EMR with Apache Spark is a powerful and highly flexible platform capable of large-scale data cleaning and feature engineering, it introduces substantially higher operational complexity, cluster management overhead, and cost compared to AWS Glue DataBrew for this use case. DataBrew is a fully managed, purpose-built service for visual data preparation that eliminates the need to provision infrastructure, write Spark code, or manage cluster scaling. When a scenario explicitly emphasizes efficiency and low operational overhead — as is the case here — DataBrew is the architecturally preferred choice over EMR for data cleaning tasks on partially populated.csv files. Keyword tip: When the scenario highlights 'efficient,' 'managed service,' or 'lower operational overhead' for data cleaning tasks, prefer AWS Glue DataBrew over Amazon EMR with Apache Spark.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to design an efficient, managed data preparation pipeline for partially structured .csv data stored in Amazon S3.

It focuses on selecting the right AWS service for each stage: schema inference, data cleaning and feature engineering, and storing the prepared dataset for ML training.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    4, 5, 3: This sequence is incorrect. Amazon Redshift Spectrum can query data in Amazon S3, but it depends on existing table metadata. It is not the correct service for automatically inferring schemas from raw .csv files. Schema discovery should be handled by AWS Glue Crawlers.

    1, 5, 3: This is the correct sequence. First, AWS Glue Crawlers infer the schema from the .csv files in Amazon S3 and populate the AWS Glue Data Catalog. Next, AWS Glue DataBrew cleans the data, handles missing values, standardizes fields, and performs feature engineering. Finally, the prepared dataset is written back to Amazon S3 for downstream ML training.

    5, 1, 3: This sequence is incorrect because data cleaning and feature engineering should not happen before schema discovery. Without inferred schema and column metadata, DataBrew cannot reliably interpret the dataset structure, column types, missing values, or transformation targets.

    1, 2, 3: This sequence is technically possible, but it is not the most efficient managed approach. Amazon EMR with Apache Spark is powerful for large-scale custom processing, but it requires more infrastructure management, Spark code, cluster configuration, and operational overhead than AWS Glue DataBrew.

Why 1, 5, 3 fits this scenario.

The clinical trial data is stored as .csv files in Amazon S3.

The files are partially populated, lack clear column labels, and contain missing values.

The organization needs to prepare the data efficiently for ML training.

The first step is schema discovery.

AWS Glue Crawlers scan the S3 data, infer schema information where possible, and register metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog.

This gives downstream services a structured understanding of the dataset.

The second step is data cleaning and feature engineering.

AWS Glue DataBrew provides a visual, low-code/no-code environment for preparing data.

It can help clean missing values, rename or organize columns, standardize formats, transform fields, and create features without requiring custom Spark jobs or cluster management.

The final step is storage.

After the data is cleaned and structured, the prepared dataset should be written back to Amazon S3.

This makes it available for downstream training workflows in services such as Amazon SageMaker.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Schema inference: Use AWS Glue Crawlers.

    Metadata registration: Populate the AWS Glue Data Catalog.

    Data cleaning: Use AWS Glue DataBrew.

    Feature engineering: Apply visual transformations and preparation recipes.

    Prepared ML dataset storage: Store the output back in Amazon S3.

    Low operational overhead: Avoid EMR when managed visual preparation is sufficient.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Raw .csv data in S3 + schema inference + low-code cleaning + ML training readiness = AWS Glue Crawlers → AWS Glue DataBrew → Amazon S3.

Remember:

    AWS Glue Crawlers: Infer schema and populate the Glue Data Catalog.

    AWS Glue DataBrew: Visual data cleaning and feature engineering.

    Amazon S3: Stores raw and prepared datasets.

    Amazon Redshift Spectrum: Queries S3 data using existing metadata; it is not the schema inference step.

    Amazon EMR: Powerful for custom Spark processing, but higher operational overhead.

    Efficient managed pipeline: Prefer purpose-built managed services when custom distributed processing is not required.

Question 32Skipped

You are a machine learning engineer at PeakPlay Analytics, a sports analytics company responsible for developing and deploying an end-to-end ML workflow for predicting athlete injury risk rates across multiple professional leagues.

The workflow involves data preprocessing, model training, hyperparameter tuning, and deployment. Additionally, the solution must support regular retraining of the model as new in-season performance data becomes available, with minimal manual intervention.

You need to select the right solution to orchestrate this workflow efficiently while ensuring scalability, reliability, and ease of management. Given these requirements, which of the following options is the MOST SUITABLE for orchestrating your ML workflow?


Correct answer
Implement the entire ML workflow using Amazon SageMaker Pipelines, which provides integrated orchestration for data processing, model training, tuning, and deployment.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Pipelines is a purpose-built, fully managed workflow orchestration service designed specifically for end-to-end ML workflows. It natively integrates data processing steps, model training jobs, hyperparameter tuning jobs, model evaluation, and deployment within a single, reproducible pipeline definition. SageMaker Pipelines also provides built-in model lineage tracking, pipeline versioning, and native integration with the SageMaker Model Registry and Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor, making it the ideal solution for automated retraining triggered by new in-season data and for enforcing CI/CD for ML at scale. Keyword tip: when a scenario requires end-to-end ML orchestration with minimal manual intervention, automated retraining, versioning, and lineage tracking on AWS, Amazon SageMaker Pipelines is the definitive service to select.
Use AWS Glue for data preprocessing, Amazon SageMaker for model training and tuning, and manually deploy the model to an Amazon EC2 instance for inference.
Explanation
Incorrect. Although using AWS Glue for data preprocessing and Amazon SageMaker for model training is technically feasible, manually deploying the trained model to an Amazon EC2 instance for inference bypasses the managed deployment, auto-scaling, and monitoring capabilities of SageMaker endpoints and SageMaker Pipelines. This hybrid approach also lacks integrated artifact tracking, automated retraining triggers, and pipeline-level automation, which are all essential when new in-season data must continuously update the model. Keyword tip: any option that concludes the ML workflow with manual model deployment to EC2 should be eliminated when scalability, automation, and managed inference are stated requirements.
Leverage Amazon EC2 instances to manually execute each step of the ML workflow, use Amazon RDS for storing intermediate results, and deploy the model using Amazon SageMaker endpoints.
Explanation
Incorrect. Manually executing each step of the ML workflow using Amazon EC2 instances and Amazon RDS is labor-intensive, error-prone, and lacks scalability. This approach requires significant operational overhead and does not provide the automation, versioning, lineage tracking, or scheduling capabilities required for reliable, repeatable ML workflows. Retraining the injury risk model each season would demand constant manual coordination, making this approach fundamentally incompatible with the minimal manual intervention requirement. Keyword tip: whenever a scenario emphasizes minimal manual intervention and automated retraining, options that require manual orchestration of compute resources are strong distractors to eliminate immediately.
Use AWS Step Functions to define and orchestrate each step of the ML workflow, integrate with Amazon SageMaker for model training and deployment, and leverage AWS Lambda for data preprocessing tasks.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Step Functions is a capable general-purpose workflow orchestration service and can integrate with Amazon SageMaker and AWS Lambda. However, building an ML-specific workflow with Step Functions requires authoring custom state machine definitions in Amazon States Language, managing inter-service integrations manually, and implementing lineage tracking and versioning from scratch. This adds substantial architectural complexity compared to Amazon SageMaker Pipelines, which is purpose-built for ML workflows and natively handles pipeline versioning, artifact lineage tracking, and CI/CD for ML without additional custom engineering. Keyword tip: Step Functions is a valid but suboptimal choice when the scenario explicitly requires an integrated, ML-native orchestration solution with built-in lineage and retraining automation.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the candidate's ability to select the most appropriate AWS service for orchestrating a complete and automated ML workflow.

It focuses on workflows that include data preprocessing, model training, hyperparameter tuning, deployment, and periodic retraining triggered by new data.

However, building a full ML workflow with Step Functions requires more custom integration, state machine design, and additional work for lineage and versioning.     Use AWS Glue for data preprocessing, Amazon SageMaker for model training and tuning, and manually deploy the model to an Amazon EC2 instance for inference: AWS Glue can handle preprocessing, and SageMaker can handle training and tuning. However, manually deploying the model to EC2 bypasses managed SageMaker deployment, monitoring, auto-scaling, and pipeline automation. This does not meet the requirement for minimal manual intervention.

    Implement the entire ML workflow using Amazon SageMaker Pipelines, which provides integrated orchestration for data processing, model training, tuning, and deployment: This is the correct option. Amazon SageMaker Pipelines is purpose-built for end-to-end ML workflow orchestration. It supports processing, training, hyperparameter tuning, evaluation, model registration, deployment, lineage tracking, and repeatable automation.

Why Amazon SageMaker Pipelines fits this scenario.

The scenario requires a complete automated ML workflow.

The workflow includes preprocessing, training, tuning, deployment, and retraining when new data arrives.

This is exactly the type of use case Amazon SageMaker Pipelines is designed to support.

Those steps can include data processing jobs, training jobs, hyperparameter tuning jobs, model evaluation logic, model registration, and deployment actions.

It also provides ML-specific capabilities that general-purpose orchestration services do not provide by default.

These include artifact lineage tracking, pipeline versioning, integration with SageMaker Model Registry, and support for CI/CD-style ML workflows.

This makes it a better fit than manual EC2 workflows, custom Step Functions state machines, or hybrid architectures that require manual deployment.

    Lineage and versioning: Use SageMaker Pipelines native tracking capabilities.

    Minimal manual intervention: Avoid manually coordinated EC2 or custom orchestration workflows.

Question 33Skipped

An ML engineer is building a generative AI application on Amazon Bedrock using large language models (LLMs) for a commercial real estate firm, PropVista Realty.


The engineer needs to understand key generative AI concepts to optimize and customize the model's performance for the application. Consider the following generative AI terms and their descriptions:

Generative AI terms:

1. Embedding
2. Token
3. Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG)
4. Temperature
5. Prompt  Descriptions:
A. A method to combine external knowledge retrieval with generative AI to produce more accurate and relevant responses.

B. Numerical representation of text (or data) in a high-dimensional vector space.

C. An input provided to a model to guide it to generate an appropriate response or output for the input.

D. Units of text (like words, subwords, or characters) processed by an LLM.

E. A parameter used to control the randomness of the model's output, influencing whether responses are more creative or deterministic.  Which option correctly matches each generative AI term to its description?


1-B, 2-D, 3-C
Explanation
Incorrect. This option incorrectly assigns description C (the definition of a Prompt — an input that guides model generation) to Retrieval Augmented Generation (3) instead of the correct description A. RAG is a technique that augments a generative model with external knowledge retrieval to improve accuracy and relevance; it is not simply an input to the model. The mappings for Embedding (1-B) and Token (2-D) are individually correct, but the erroneous assignment of RAG to description C makes this option invalid overall. Keyword tip: RAG is defined by its retrieval-plus-generation architecture using external knowledge bases — do not confuse it with a Prompt, which is merely the input text supplied to the model to direct its response.
4-C, 5-E, 3-A
Explanation
Incorrect. This option addresses only terms 3, 4, and 5, leaving terms 1 (Embedding) and 2 (Token) entirely unmatched, and therefore does not constitute a complete answer for the full set of terms presented in the question. While the individual pairings cited — Temperature (4) mapped to E and Prompt (5) mapped to C — are independently accurate, a partial mapping that omits required terms cannot be the correct answer. Keyword tip: Watch for distractor options on MLA-C01 that contain some correct individual pairings but deliberately omit required terms, making the overall match incomplete and invalid.
Correct answer
1-B, 2-D, 3-A
Explanation
Correct. This option correctly maps all three terms: Embedding (1) is a numerical representation of text in a high-dimensional vector space (B), which enables semantic similarity comparisons essential for tasks such as property search and document clustering in a real estate context; Token (2) refers to units of text — such as words, subwords, or characters — that an LLM processes as its fundamental input/output units (D); and Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) (3) is a technique that integrates external data retrieval with generative AI to produce more accurate and contextually relevant outputs (A). All three pairings are independently correct and together constitute the only fully valid matching among the options presented. Keyword tip: On MLA-C01, remember that embeddings enable vector similarity search, tokens are the atomic units of LLM input and output, and RAG extends a model's knowledge by grounding responses in externally retrieved documents.
1-D, 2-B, 3-A
Explanation
Incorrect. This option transposes the descriptions of Embedding and Token: it assigns description D (units of text processed by an LLM) to Embedding (1) and description B (numerical representation in a high-dimensional vector space) to Token (2), which reverses their correct definitions. Embeddings are continuous, dense vector representations of semantic meaning, whereas tokens are discrete textual units that serve as the atomic elements an LLM reads and generates. The mapping for RAG (3-A) is correct, but the swapped definitions for Embedding and Token make this option invalid. Keyword tip: On MLA-C01, remember that embeddings are dense, continuous vector representations used for semantic similarity, while tokens are the discrete textual units — words, subwords, or characters — that LLMs process sequentially during inference.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests foundational generative AI terminology as applied in the context of Amazon Bedrock and LLM-based applications.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The scenario requires understanding the precise distinction between embeddings, tokens, Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG), temperature, and prompts.

The concepts at a glance.

    1-B, 2-D, 3-A: This option correctly maps the terms. Embedding is a numerical representation of text or data in a high-dimensional vector space. Token refers to the units of text processed by an LLM, such as words, subwords, or characters. Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) combines external knowledge retrieval with generative AI to produce more accurate and relevant responses.

    4-C, 5-E, 3-A: This option is incomplete. It addresses only temperature, prompt, and RAG, while leaving embedding and token unmatched. Even though some individual mappings are correct, the option does not fully answer the question.

    1-B, 2-D, 3-C: This option incorrectly maps RAG to the definition of a prompt. A prompt is the input provided to a model to guide its response. RAG is not simply an input; it is an architecture that retrieves external information and uses that context to improve generation.

    1-D, 2-B, 3-A: This option reverses the definitions of embedding and token. Tokens are discrete text units processed by an LLM. Embeddings are dense vector representations that capture semantic meaning.

Why 1-B, 2-D, 3-A fits this scenario.

The option 1-B, 2-D, 3-A is correct because each term is matched to its proper definition.

Embedding maps to B, because an embedding is a numerical representation of text or data in a high-dimensional vector space.

Embeddings are commonly used for semantic similarity, vector search, document retrieval, and recommendation systems.

Token maps to D, because tokens are the units of text processed by an LLM.

A token can represent a word, part of a word, punctuation, or another subword unit depending on the tokenizer.

Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) maps to A, because RAG combines information retrieval with generative AI.

In a RAG architecture, the application retrieves relevant external documents or knowledge sources and provides that context to the model so it can generate more accurate and grounded responses.

This is why the correct mapping is:

    1-B: Embedding equals numerical vector representation.

    2-D: Token equals unit of text processed by an LLM.

    3-A: RAG equals retrieval plus generation using external knowledge.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Embedding = vector representation.

Token = text unit processed by an LLM.

RAG = retrieval plus generation.

Remember:

    Embedding: Used for semantic similarity and vector search.

    Token: The unit an LLM reads and generates.

    RAG: Grounds model responses using retrieved external data.

    Temperature: Controls randomness or creativity in model output.

    Prompt: The input that guides the model response.

Question 34Skipped

A property and casualty insurance company, PinnacleGuard Insurance, uses Amazon SageMaker to develop and register machine learning (ML) models for various business needs, such as claims fraud detection, underwriting risk assessment, and policyholder segmentation.


These models are stored in model groups within the SageMaker Model Registry.

The data science team is organized into three specialized groups based on their areas of focus: fraud detection models, risk assessment models, and policyholder segmentation models.


An ML engineer needs to implement a solution to organize the existing models into these three business categories to improve discoverability at scale, while ensuring the integrity of the model artifacts and their existing groupings remains unaffected.


Which solution will meet these requirements?


Correct answer
Use SageMaker Model Registry collections to group existing model groups into high-level categories, such as fraud detection, risk assessment, and policyholder segmentation.
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Model Registry collections allow users to logically organize existing model groups into high-level business categories without modifying, moving, or disrupting the underlying model groups, their artifacts, approval statuses, or lineage metadata. Collections are specifically designed to improve discoverability at scale and support hierarchical organization of model groups across a large registry. They can be extended seamlessly as new models and business categories are added over time. Keyword tip: Whenever a scenario requires non-disruptive, scalable categorization of model groups in SageMaker Model Registry while preserving existing groupings and lineage, SageMaker Model Registry collections is the correct answer.
Use SageMaker Feature Store to tag models with metadata for fraud detection, risk assessment, and policyholder segmentation. Filter models using queries on the Feature Store.
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Feature Store is a purpose-built service for storing, sharing, and serving ML features used during model training and inference — it is not designed to manage model group organization or model discoverability within the Model Registry. Using Feature Store for model metadata management represents an architectural misuse of the service, conflating feature management with model management. Keyword tip: SageMaker Feature Store is for feature data management, not model management — always distinguish between feature management (Feature Store) and model lifecycle management (Model Registry, model collections).
Attach custom tags to each model artifact in the SageMaker Model Registry to specify their category and filter models based on tags.
Explanation
Incorrect. While custom tags in the SageMaker Model Registry can add useful metadata to individual model artifacts, they do not provide a hierarchical organizational structure. Tags enable filtering but lack the ability to logically group model groups into higher-level business categories at scale, making them insufficient as a standalone solution when categorical, non-disruptive organization is required. Keyword tip: When the requirement specifies organizing model groups into categories without disrupting existing groupings, tags alone are insufficient — look for a purpose-built grouping feature such as SageMaker Model Registry collections.
Move models into newly created SageMaker model groups for fraud detection, risk assessment, and policyholder segmentation, reassigning them from their current groups.
Explanation
Incorrect. Physically moving models into newly created model groups would disrupt the existing integrity of the registry, including model lineage, approval workflows, version history, and associated metadata tied to the original model groups. This approach directly violates the requirement that existing groupings and model artifact integrity must remain unaffected. Keyword tip: Any solution that involves reassigning or migrating models to new model groups is destructive to lineage and approval state — eliminate it when the requirement explicitly states that existing groupings must be preserved.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of SageMaker Model Registry organizational capabilities.

It focuses on the distinction between custom tags, physical model group reassignment, and Model Registry collections.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Attach custom tags to each model artifact in the SageMaker Model Registry to specify their category and filter models based on tags: Custom tags can add useful metadata to individual model artifacts. However, tags do not provide a hierarchical organizational structure. They help with filtering, but they do not logically group model groups into high-level business categories at scale.

    Use SageMaker Model Registry collections to group existing model groups into high-level categories, such as fraud detection, risk assessment, and policyholder segmentation: This is the correct option. SageMaker Model Registry collections allow users to logically organize existing model groups into high-level categories without moving, modifying, or disrupting the underlying model groups, model artifacts, approval statuses, or lineage metadata.

    Use SageMaker Feature Store to tag models with metadata for fraud detection, risk assessment, and policyholder segmentation. Filter models using queries on the Feature Store: SageMaker Feature Store is designed to store, share, and serve ML features used during training and inference. It is not designed to organize models or manage discoverability inside the SageMaker Model Registry.

    Move models into newly created SageMaker model groups for fraud detection, risk assessment, and policyholder segmentation, reassigning them from their current groups: Physically moving models into new model groups would disrupt the existing organization of the registry. It could affect lineage, approval workflows, version history, and metadata tied to the original model groups. This violates the requirement to preserve existing groupings and artifact integrity.

Why SageMaker Model Registry collections fit this scenario.

SageMaker Model Registry collections are the best fit when existing model groups need to be organized into high-level business categories.

Collections provide a logical grouping layer.

They do not require moving model versions out of their existing model groups.

They also preserve the underlying model lineage, approval status, version history, and metadata.

This makes collections useful when an organization has many model groups and needs better discoverability at scale.

For example, models can be grouped into categories such as fraud detection, risk assessment, and policyholder segmentation without restructuring the registry.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Non-disruptive organization: Use Model Registry collections.

    High-level business categorization: Use Model Registry collections.

Question 35Skipped

A data scientist at GreenHarvest Cooperative is developing a deep learning model to classify field survey images for crop disease detection across member farms.

The model initially shows high accuracy on the training data but performs poorly on the validation set, indicating signs of overfitting.

The dataset is limited in size due to the seasonal and regional nature of the collected imagery, and the model is complex, with many parameters. To improve generalization and reduce overfitting, appropriate techniques must be implemented while balancing model complexity and performance.

Given these challenges, which combination of techniques is MOST LIKELY to help prevent overfitting and improve the model's performance on unseen data?
Use ensembling by combining multiple versions of the same model trained with different random seeds, and apply data augmentation to artificially increase the size of the dataset
Explanation
Incorrect. Ensembling and data augmentation are individually powerful anti-overfitting techniques, but the specific ensembling strategy described here — combining multiple versions of the same model trained with different random seeds — introduces only marginal diversity. Because the model architecture, training data, and hyperparameters remain identical across ensemble members, the individual models will exhibit highly correlated predictions and errors, yielding limited generalization improvement over a single model. Effective ensembling requires diversity in model architecture, training subsets, or feature representations. The absence of early stopping also means each individual model may still overfit significantly before ensembling is applied. Keyword tip: 'same model architecture + different random seeds' ensembling is a weaker strategy than diverse-model ensembling, and any option omitting early stopping leaves unchecked overtraining as a risk.
Apply early stopping to halt training when the validation loss stops improving, and use dropout as a regularization technique to prevent the model from becoming too reliant on specific neurons
Explanation
Incorrect. Early stopping and dropout are both valid and widely used regularization techniques in deep learning. Early stopping terminates training when the validation loss stops improving, and dropout randomly deactivates neurons during training to reduce co-adaptation between units. However, neither technique addresses the fundamental constraint of having a limited and low-diversity dataset. Without increasing data diversity through augmentation or leveraging variance reduction through diverse model ensembling, these two techniques alone provide an incomplete solution compared to option 1, which also incorporates data augmentation and ensembling. Keyword tip: 'early stopping + dropout only' is a common distractor when the scenario explicitly mentions limited training data — the correct answer will typically include data augmentation to compensate for dataset scarcity.
Correct answer
Combine data augmentation to increase the diversity of the training data with early stopping to prevent overfitting, and use ensembling to average predictions from multiple models
Explanation
Correct. This combination directly addresses both root causes of the overfitting problem: limited data and model complexity. Data augmentation artificially expands the training dataset by generating diverse variants of existing samples — such as rotated, flipped, or color-shifted crop images — which is especially critical when the dataset is small and seasonally constrained. Early stopping prevents the model from memorizing training noise by halting training when validation loss ceases to improve. Ensembling further improves generalization by averaging predictions across multiple independently trained models, reducing the probability that overfitting in any single model dominates the final output. Together, these three complementary techniques form the most robust and comprehensive solution for the described scenario. Keyword tip: when a question pairs 'limited dataset + complex deep learning model + overfitting', look for the combination of data augmentation + early stopping + ensembling as the most complete multi-pronged answer.
Prune the model by removing less important layers and nodes, and use L2 regularization to reduce the magnitude of the model's weights, preventing overfitting
Explanation
Incorrect. While pruning reduces model complexity by removing low-importance weights or layers, and L2 regularization penalizes large weight values to stabilize learning, this combination does not directly address the limited dataset problem. Pruning without careful validation can lead to underfitting by removing parameters that were still contributing to representational capacity. L2 regularization alone is insufficient to compensate for the lack of data diversity inherent in a small, seasonally collected image corpus. This pairing is more appropriate for reducing inference latency or model size after training, not as a primary anti-overfitting strategy for small datasets. Keyword tip: 'pruning + L2 regularization' typically targets model compression and weight stability rather than dataset scarcity — eliminate this option when limited data is an explicit constraint.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to select a comprehensive strategy to reduce overfitting in deep learning models.

It focuses on scenarios where both limited training data and high model complexity are contributing to poor generalization.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Combine data augmentation to increase the diversity of the training data with early stopping to prevent overfitting, and use ensembling to average predictions from multiple models: This is the correct option. Data augmentation increases training data diversity. Early stopping prevents the model from continuing to train after validation performance stops improving. Ensembling reduces variance by combining predictions from multiple models.

    Prune the model by removing less important layers and nodes, and use L2 regularization to reduce the magnitude of the model's weights, preventing overfitting: Pruning and L2 regularization can reduce model complexity, but they do not directly solve the limited-data problem. Pruning is often more useful for compression and inference optimization, while L2 helps control weight magnitude.

    Apply early stopping to halt training when the validation loss stops improving, and use dropout as a regularization technique to prevent the model from becoming too reliant on specific neurons: Early stopping and dropout are valid anti-overfitting techniques. However, this option does not address the lack of training data diversity. It is a good partial strategy, but not the most complete answer.

    Use ensembling by combining multiple versions of the same model trained with different random seeds, and apply data augmentation to artificially increase the size of the dataset: Data augmentation is useful, and ensembling can help. However, if all ensemble members use the same architecture, same training data, and same hyperparameters, their errors may be highly correlated. The absence of early stopping also leaves each model vulnerable to overtraining.

Why data augmentation, early stopping, and ensembling fit this scenario.

The scenario describes a deep learning model that is overfitting.

It also includes two major constraints:

    The dataset is limited.

    The model is complex.

A strong answer must address both issues.

Data augmentation helps with the limited-data problem.

For image-based models, this can include transformations such as rotations, flips, crops, brightness changes, or color shifts.

These transformations create realistic variations of existing examples, helping the model learn more general patterns instead of memorizing the training data.

Early stopping helps control overtraining.

It monitors validation performance and stops training when validation loss no longer improves.

This prevents the model from continuing to memorize noise in the training set.

Ensembling helps reduce prediction variance.

By combining predictions from multiple models, the final output becomes less dependent on the behavior of any single overfit model.

Together, these techniques form a complete anti-overfitting strategy:

    Data augmentation: Increases data diversity.

    Early stopping: Prevents excessive training.

    Ensembling: Improves generalization by reducing variance.

    Limited dataset problem: Addressed by augmentation.

    Model complexity problem: Addressed by early stopping and ensembling.

This is stronger than using only regularization, pruning, dropout, or hyperparameter changes.

Those techniques can help, but they do not address both root causes as completely.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Limited dataset + complex deep learning model + overfitting = data augmentation, early stopping, and ensembling.

Remember:

    Data augmentation: Best when the dataset is small or lacks diversity.

    Early stopping: Stops training when validation performance stops improving.

    Ensembling: Reduces variance by combining multiple models.

    Dropout: Regularizes neural networks by reducing neuron co-adaptation.

    L2 regularization: Penalizes large weights.

    Pruning: Reduces model size and complexity, often useful for compression.

    Synthetic data: Must be validated carefully to avoid distributional bias.

Official AWS references.

    What is overfitting?

    Object2Vec hyperparameters.

    Training parameters.

Key exam terms: overfitting, data augmentation, early stopping, ensembling, dropout, L2 regularization, model pruning, limited dataset, deep learning generalization, validation loss.

Question 36Skipped

A renewable energy company, SolarGrid Analytics, is building a machine learning platform that requires a centralized repository to store, share, and manage inputs to ML models used during both training and inference.

Which AWS service is specifically designed to fulfill this requirement?
Correct answer
Amazon SageMaker Feature Store
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Feature Store is a fully managed, purpose-built repository designed to store, share, and manage features for ML models. Features are the inputs to ML models used during both training and real-time inference, and Feature Store provides both an online store (low-latency retrieval) and an offline store (batch access via Amazon S3) to support both use cases. It supports ingestion from diverse sources such as Amazon S3, Amazon Redshift, AWS Lake Formation, Snowflake, and Databricks Delta Lake, making it well suited for centralized feature management across teams and pipelines. Keyword tip: whenever a question describes a centralized, reusable feature repository that must serve both training jobs and live inference endpoints, Amazon SageMaker Feature Store is the correct answer.
Amazon SageMaker Clarify
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Clarify helps detect potential bias in datasets and model predictions and provides model explainability reports without requiring custom code. It analyzes specified input features — such as sensor readings or geographic attributes — to identify disparate impacts and generate SHAP-based feature importance scores. It does not function as a feature repository and does not manage feature storage or retrieval for ML model inputs. Keyword tip: Clarify is the correct choice for questions involving bias detection, fairness analysis, or model explainability — not feature storage or sharing.
Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth is a human-in-the-loop data labeling and annotation service that enables data generation, annotation, model review, and evaluation through managed or self-service workflows using a workforce of human annotators. It produces labeled datasets as outputs but does not store or manage engineered model input features for use during training or inference. Keyword tip: Ground Truth is the correct choice when a question involves human labeling of raw data, data annotation pipelines, or model evaluation with human feedback — not feature lifecycle management.
Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler is a data preparation and feature engineering tool that reduces the time needed to aggregate, transform, and prepare tabular and image data for ML — from weeks to minutes. While it can export feature definitions and integrate with Feature Store, it is itself a data transformation and exploration environment, not a persistent repository for storing and sharing features across training and inference pipelines. Keyword tip: Data Wrangler is associated with visual data preparation, cleansing, transformation, and exploratory analysis workflows — not centralized feature storage or retrieval during inference.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the candidate's ability to distinguish between purpose-built Amazon SageMaker services across the ML lifecycle.

It specifically tests whether the candidate can identify the correct service for centralized feature management versus services used for data preparation, labeling, or governance.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Amazon SageMaker Feature Store: Amazon SageMaker Feature Store is a fully managed, purpose-built repository for storing, sharing, and managing features for ML models. Features are the inputs used by models during both training and real-time inference. Feature Store provides an online store for low-latency feature retrieval and an offline store for batch access through Amazon S3.

    Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler: Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler is used for data preparation, exploration, transformation, and feature engineering. It can help create features and can integrate with Feature Store, but it is not itself a persistent feature repository.

    Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth: Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth is a human-in-the-loop data labeling and annotation service. It helps create labeled datasets, but it does not store or manage engineered features for training and inference.

    Amazon SageMaker Clarify: Amazon SageMaker Clarify is used for bias detection and model explainability. It can analyze features and generate explainability reports, but it does not function as a centralized feature repository.

Why Amazon SageMaker Feature Store fits this scenario.

Amazon SageMaker Feature Store is the correct answer because it is specifically designed to store, discover, share, and manage ML features.

It supports both training and inference use cases.

The online store provides low-latency access to features for real-time predictions.

The offline store supports batch access for model training, analysis, and historical feature reuse.

This dual-store design makes Feature Store the correct service when a scenario requires a centralized feature layer that can serve both real-time inference endpoints and batch training workflows.

It also helps teams reuse consistent feature definitions across multiple models and pipelines.

This is why Feature Store must be distinguished from adjacent SageMaker services:

    Feature Store: Stores and serves reusable ML features.

    Data Wrangler: Prepares and transforms data.

    Ground Truth: Labels data.

    Clarify: Detects bias and explains model behavior.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Centralized reusable feature repository + training and real-time inference = Amazon SageMaker Feature Store.

Remember:

    Low-latency online feature lookup: Use Feature Store online store.

    Batch training feature access: Use Feature Store offline store.

    Data transformation and feature engineering: Use Data Wrangler.

    Human data labeling: Use Ground Truth.

    Bias detection and explainability: Use Clarify.

Official AWS references.

    Amazon SageMaker Feature Store.

    Amazon SageMaker Ground Truth.

    Amazon SageMaker Clarify.

Question 37Skipped

A pharmaceutical research organization, GenomePath Biosciences, has deployed a machine learning (ML) model using Amazon SageMaker to forecast compound synthesis demand across its internal laboratory management systems.

The model is exposed via a SageMaker endpoint that processes requests from multiple internal applications.


The organization needs to record and monitor all API call events made to the endpoint and receive a notification whenever the number of requests exceeds a specific threshold during peak processing hours. Which solution will meet these requirements?


Use Amazon EventBridge to capture SageMaker API call events and configure a rule to send a notification when the event count breaches the threshold
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon EventBridge is designed to capture and route discrete event patterns — such as SageMaker training job state-change events or pipeline execution status changes — to downstream targets. It does not aggregate continuous numeric metrics over time, does not support threshold comparisons against cumulative invocation counts, and cannot function as a metric alarm system. For threshold-based alerting on API call volume, CloudWatch Alarms are required instead. Keyword tip: EventBridge is appropriate for event-driven routing of discrete state-change events, not for continuous metric-based threshold alarms on endpoint invocation rates.
Use SageMaker Model Monitor to capture and analyze endpoint traffic and configure a rule to notify when API calls exceed the specified threshold
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Model Monitor is purpose-built for detecting data quality degradation, model bias, feature attribution drift, and model quality issues by analyzing the statistical properties of endpoint input and output data. It does not collect or evaluate raw API invocation counts or request rates, and it provides no native mechanism to trigger an alert when the number of API calls exceeds a numeric threshold within a time window. Using Model Monitor for infrastructure-level traffic volume alerting would be a misapplication of the service. Keyword tip: SageMaker Model Monitor targets data drift, model quality, and bias detection — not infrastructure-level traffic volume or invocation rate threshold alerting.
Correct answer
Use Amazon CloudWatch to monitor the API call metrics for the SageMaker endpoint and create an alarm to send notifications through Amazon SNS when the call count breaches the threshold
Explanation
Correct. Amazon CloudWatch natively collects invocation metrics for SageMaker endpoints — including Invocations, InvocationErrors, and ModelLatency — without requiring additional configuration. You can create a CloudWatch Alarm that continuously evaluates a chosen metric against a defined numeric threshold and, when the threshold is breached, publishes a notification to an Amazon SNS topic. This CloudWatch Alarms + SNS pattern is the canonical AWS solution for threshold-based alerting on SageMaker endpoint traffic volume and directly satisfies both the monitoring and notification requirements stated in the scenario. Keyword tip: whenever a question requires evaluating a quantitative operational metric over time and triggering an automated notification when a numeric threshold is crossed on a SageMaker endpoint, CloudWatch Alarms integrated with Amazon SNS is the primary MLA-C01 answer.
Enable AWS CloudTrail to log all SageMaker API call events and use CloudTrail Insights to send notifications when the API call volume exceeds a threshold
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS CloudTrail records control-plane API calls made to AWS services for auditing, governance, and compliance purposes, capturing who called which API and when. CloudTrail Insights can detect anomalous write-API activity patterns but is designed for security auditing rather than real-time, threshold-based metric alarms on data-plane invocation volume. It does not provide a direct mechanism to trigger an SNS notification when a specific numeric invocation count threshold is crossed within a defined monitoring window. Keyword tip: CloudTrail is for control-plane audit logging and anomaly detection, not for continuous data-plane performance metric monitoring with configurable numeric thresholds.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to select the correct AWS monitoring and alerting pattern for Amazon SageMaker endpoint traffic.

It focuses on distinguishing metric-based monitoring, event routing, audit logging, and ML-specific model monitoring.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use Amazon CloudWatch to monitor the API call metrics for the SageMaker endpoint and create an alarm to send notifications through Amazon SNS when the call count breaches the threshold: This is the correct option. Amazon CloudWatch collects operational metrics for SageMaker endpoints, including invocation-related metrics. CloudWatch Alarms can evaluate a metric against a threshold and send notifications through Amazon SNS.

    Use Amazon EventBridge to capture SageMaker API call events and configure a rule to send a notification when the event count breaches the threshold: Amazon EventBridge routes events based on event patterns. It is not the primary service for evaluating continuous numeric metrics over time or creating threshold-based alarms on endpoint invocation counts.

    Use SageMaker Model Monitor to capture and analyze endpoint traffic and configure a rule to notify when API calls exceed the specified threshold: SageMaker Model Monitor is used to monitor data quality, model quality, bias drift, and feature attribution drift. It is not designed to alert on raw API call count thresholds.

    Enable AWS CloudTrail to log all SageMaker API call events and use CloudTrail Insights to send notifications when the API call volume exceeds a threshold: AWS CloudTrail records API activity for auditing, governance, and compliance. It is not the correct service for real-time operational threshold alerts on SageMaker endpoint invocation volume.

Why CloudWatch Alarms with Amazon SNS fit this scenario.

The scenario requires monitoring endpoint traffic.

The key requirement is to detect when API call volume crosses a defined threshold.

That is a metric-based alerting problem.

Amazon CloudWatch is the AWS service used to collect and evaluate operational metrics.

For SageMaker endpoints, CloudWatch can track metrics such as endpoint invocations, invocation errors, and latency.

A CloudWatch Alarm can evaluate one of these metrics over a defined time period.

When the metric breaches the threshold, the alarm can publish a notification to an Amazon SNS topic.

This allows the operations or ML team to receive automated alerts when endpoint traffic exceeds the expected volume.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Metric monitoring: Use Amazon CloudWatch.

    Threshold evaluation: Use CloudWatch Alarms.

    Automated notification: Use Amazon SNS.

    SageMaker endpoint traffic: Monitor invocation-related metrics.

    Operational alerting: Alert when traffic volume exceeds a defined threshold.

    Avoid service misuse: Do not use EventBridge, CloudTrail, or Model Monitor for numeric metric threshold alarms.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

SageMaker endpoint metric + numeric threshold + notification = CloudWatch Alarm with Amazon SNS.

Remember:

    CloudWatch: Operational metrics and alarms.

    CloudWatch Alarms: Threshold-based alerting.

    Amazon SNS: Notification delivery.

    EventBridge: Event routing, not metric threshold evaluation.

    CloudTrail: API audit logging, not endpoint traffic alarms.

    SageMaker Model Monitor: ML data and model quality monitoring, not raw request-count alerting.

Official AWS references.

    Monitor Amazon SageMaker with Amazon CloudWatch.

Key exam terms: Amazon CloudWatch, CloudWatch Alarms, Amazon SNS, SageMaker endpoint invocation metrics, threshold-based alerting, Amazon EventBridge, SageMaker Model Monitor, AWS CloudTrail.
